English Original

3. Negative Impacts on the Immigration System and Policy Objectives a. Purpose and Need for Rulemaking Comments: Numerous commenters argued that DHS had failed to provide sufficient evidence that the current D/S framework leads to widespread noncompliance, fraud, or abuse. They stated that DHS relied on anecdotal examples and isolated cases rather than comprehensive data demonstrating systemic problems to justify the call for significant policy change. Commenters expressed that the rule did not draw a rational connection between alleged systemic fraud and abuse within the student visa program and the proposed changes, and that the proposed rule is a solution to a problem that does not exist. Others questioned how limiting the period of admission and requiring additional Form I-539 burden on students would address fraud or abuse. A commenter remarked that the NPRM lacks evidence demonstrating that eliminating D/S would have prevented any of the cited security incidents. The commenters continued stating that DHS overstated the justification of misuse and that the current system provides adequate oversight without imposing new burdens. Commenters specifically highlighted the lack of evidence for abuse in the affected nonimmigrant categories. Commenters said that the increased burden on F-1 students is a stark contrast to the small amount of abuse. Multiple commenters stated that DHS provided no evidence of visa overstays specific to J-1 physicians, who they described as carefully vetted, monitored, and supervised. Commenters acknowledged the seriousness of examples cited by DHS, such as those from the NIH study, but questioned how requiring J-1 exchange visitors to file extension paperwork with USCIS would mitigate these issues. Similarly, many commenters noted that DHS provided no evidence of abuse or fraud in the I visa category for foreign journalists, with some suggesting that DHS justified including the I visa category using parity arguments rather than finding evidence of fraud and abuse. Commenters expressed concern that changes to the I visa might be politically motivated, as DHS did not identify compliance problems with this visa category. Response: DHS disagrees with the general premise of these comments. While DHS provided specific examples of fraud, national security concerns, and nonimmigrant students remaining in the United States for lengthy periods of time under the D/S framework, DHS's intent and rationale for this rulemaking was explained in detail in the proposed rule's preamble. Such concerns, as highlighted in the NPRM, demonstrate the fraud, national security, and other issues associated with the D/S framework. In the preamble to the proposed rule, DHS pointed out that admission for D/S does not afford immigration officers enough predetermined opportunities to directly verify that aliens granted such nonimmigrant statuses are engaging only in those activities their respective classifications authorize while they are in the United States. This has undermined DHS's ability to effectively enforce compliance with statutory inadmissibility grounds related to unlawful presence and has created national security vulnerabilities and incentives for fraud and abuse. DHS believes that this rule will provide DHS with the mechanisms to address some of the concerns surrounding the integrity of F, J, and I programs. With respect to J classifications, DHS indicated that the national security risks posed by D/S admissions for individuals admitted under the J classification are similar to those posed by the F classification.[117] DHS provided examples of several cases involving J-1 students involved in fraud and espionage, demonstrating the fraud, national security, and other issues associated with the D/S framework. As noted by several commenters, DHS cited a report prepared by a panel of experts commissioned by the NIH to study foreign influence on federally-funded scientific research, which stated that “[s]mall numbers of scientists have committed serious violations of NIH policies and systems by not disclosing foreign support ( i.e., grants), laboratories, or funded faculty positions in other countries.” [118] As with F nonimmigrants, setting the length of the J nonimmigrant's specific program to not exceed a 4-year period will establish a mechanism for immigration officers to assess these nonimmigrants at defined periods (such as when applying for an EOS in the United States beyond a 4-year admission period) and determine whether the J-1 nonimmigrants are complying with the conditions of their classification. This will increase vetting of the J nonimmigrant population, which will help to prevent and deter nefarious actors. DHS believes that this rule will enhance DHS's ability to detect fraud, abuse, and national security issues as well as take preventive action to secure the nation. With respect to I nonimmigrant visas, DHS recognizes the lack of public data cited in the proposed rule regarding fraud and overstays specific to I nonimmigrants. However, DHS disagrees with the general premise of these comments. The vulnerabilities generally associated with the D/S admission are not unique to F and J nonimmigrant classifications, and the lack of public data for the I nonimmigrant classification does not mean that the vulnerabilities are absent for that classification. As explained in the preamble of the NPRM and in this final rule, one of the goals of this rule is to strengthen vetting and information collection and help immigration officers ensure that the I nonimmigrants are, and will be, engaged in activities that are permissible under INA 101(a)(15)(I), and do not pose national security issues (in addition to the F and J nonimmigrant classifications). As previously mentioned, DHS believes that admitting aliens temporarily in the United States for a fixed period will encourage aliens to maintain lawful status and prevent or reduce instances in which nonimmigrants unlawfully remain in the United States after their activities or assignments end.

中文翻译

本节汇总了公众针对拟议规则提出的关于对移民系统与政策目标的负面影响（特别是规则的目的与必要性缺乏依据）的质疑，以及国土安全部（DHS）的官方回应：


1. 公众的主要质疑：


* 缺乏系统性证据： 许多评论者认为，DHS 未能提供充分的综合数据来证明现行的 D/S 框架导致了广泛的违规、欺诈或滥用，DHS 仅依赖于个别孤立的轶事案例，属于“为不存在的问题寻找解决方案”。


* 质疑因果关系与表格负担： 评论者质疑，限制入境期限并要求学生填写额外的 I-539 表格究竟如何能防止欺诈、间谍活动或逾期滞留，并指出 NPRM 中缺乏证据表明取消 D/S 能够阻止所引用的安全事件。


* 针对特定类别的质疑：


* J-1 类别： 评论者指出 DHS 没有提供 J-1 医生（经过严格审查监督）逾期滞留的具体证据，且质疑要求 J-1 学者提交延期纸质文件如何能解决 NIH 报告中提到的外国支持未披露问题。


* I-1 类别： 评论者指出 DHS 缺乏关于外媒记者欺诈或逾期滞留的公开数据，质疑将 I 签证纳入其中纯粹是出于“对等（parity）”或政治动机，而非基于实际违规证据。


2. DHS 的官方回应：


* 重申 D/S 制度的根本漏洞： DHS 驳斥了“缺乏证据”的说法，重申 D/S 制度本身没有为移民官提供足够的预定时间点来直接核查非移民的合规性，从而削弱了执法能力并滋生了国家安全漏洞。


* 关于 J 类风险的依据： DHS 援引 NIH 专家小组关于科学研究中未披露外国支持的报告，以及具体的 J-1 欺诈和间谍案例，说明设定最长 4 年的固定期限并结合 EOS 审核，能够有效增加对 J 类人群的审查并震慑恶意行为者。


* 关于 I 类类别的回应： DHS 承认在提议中针对 I 类非移民具体欺诈和逾期滞留的公开数据较少，但强调 D/S 带来的漏洞并非 F 和 J 类所独有。通过将 I 类纳入固定期限管理，能够统一强化信息收集和资格审查，确保媒体代表真正从事 INA 101(a)(15)(I) 准许的活动并不构成安全威胁。


详细翻译


3. 对移民系统和政策目标的负面影响


a. 规则制定的目的与必要性


意见： 许多评论者认为，DHS 未能提供充分的证据证明当前的 D/S 框架会导致广泛的不合规、欺诈或滥用。他们表示，DHS 依赖的是轶事示例和孤立案例，而不是能够证明系统性问题的综合数据，以此来证明进行重大政策变更的正当性。评论者表示，该规则并未在学生签证项目内所谓的系统性欺诈与滥用之间建立理性的联系，并且拟议规则是在为一个并不存在的问题寻找解决方案。其他人则质疑限制入境期限并对学生强加额外的 Form I-539 负担将如何解决欺诈或滥用问题。一位评论者指出，NPRM 缺乏证据表明取消 D/S 本可以阻止任何被引用的安全事件。评论者继续表示，DHS 夸大了滥用的理由，且当前的系统在不强加新负担的情况下提供了充足的监督。


评论者特别强调了在受影响的非移民类别中缺乏滥用证据。评论者表示，对 F-1 学生增加的负担与少量的滥用形成了鲜明对比。多位评论者表示，DHS 没有提供针对 J-1 医生的签证逾期滞留的证据，他们将 J-1 医生描述为经过仔细审查、监控和监督的人员。评论者承认 DHS 引用的示例的严重性，例如 NIH 研究中的示例，但质疑要求 J-1 交流访问者向 USCIS 提交延期纸质文件将如何减轻这些问题。同样，许多评论者指出，DHS 没有提供外国记者 I 签证类别中存在滥用或欺诈的证据，一些人暗示 DHS 将 I 签证类别纳入其中是出于对等论点，而不是发现了欺诈和滥用的证据。评论者表示担心，对 I 签证的更改可能是出于政治动机，因为 DHS 并未发现该签证类别存在合规问题。


回应： DHS 不同意这些评论的一般前提。虽然 DHS 提供了欺诈、国家安全关切以及非移民学生在 D/S 框架下在美停留较长时间的具体示例，但 DHS 针对此次规则制定的意图和基本原理已在拟议规则的序言中进行了详细解释。正如 NPRM 中所强调的那样，此类关切证明了与 D/S 框架相关的欺诈、国家安全和其他问题。在拟议规则的序言中，DHS 指出，D/S 准入并没有为移民官员提供足够预定的机会，以直接核实获得此类非移民身份的外籍人员在美期间是否仅从事其各自类别授权的活动。这削弱了 DHS 有效强制执行与非法滞留相关的法定不得入境理由的能力，并造成了国家安全漏洞以及欺诈和滥用的动机。DHS 相信，该规则将为 DHS 提供解决围绕 F、J 和 I 项目完整性的部分关切的机制。


关于 J 类身份，DHS 表明，J 类身份入境人员的 D/S 入境所构成的国家安全风险与 F 类相似 [117]。DHS 提供了数个涉及 J-1 学生参与欺诈和间谍活动的案件示例，证明了与 D/S 框架相关的欺诈、国家安全和其他问题。正如几位评论者所注意到的那样，DHS 引用了一份由 NIH 委托专家组编写的研究外国对联邦资助科学研究影响的报告，该报告指出：“少数科学家严重违反了 NIH 的政策和系统，未披露国外支持（即资助）、实验室或在其他国家的受资助教职职位。” [118]


与 F 类非移民一样，将 J 类非移民特定项目的长度设定为不超过 4 年的期限，将为移民官员建立一个机制，以便在确定的时期（例如在境内申请超过 4 年入境期限的 EOS 时）评估这些非移民，并确定 J-1 非移民是否遵守其身份条件。这将增加对 J 类非移民人口的审查，从而有助于预防和震慑邪恶演员。DHS 相信，该规则将增强 DHS 检测欺诈、滥用和国家安全问题的能力，并采取预防行动以保卫国家。


关于 I 类非移民签证，DHS 承认拟议规则中未引述有关 I 类非移民欺诈和逾期滞留的公开数据。然而，DHS 不同意这些评论的一般前提。通常与 D/S 准入相关的漏洞并非 F 和 J 类非移民身份所独有，缺乏针对 I 类非移民的公开数据并不意味着该分类不存在这些漏洞。正如 NPRM 序言和本最终规则中所解释的那样，该规则的目标之一是加强审查和信息收集，并帮助移民官员确保 I 类非移民现在和将来都在从事 INA 101(a)(15)(I) 准许的活动，且不存在国家安全问题（除 F 和 J 类非移民身份外）。如前所述，DHS 认为，临时准许外籍人员以固定期限入境美国将鼓励外籍人员保持合法身份，并防止或减少非移民在其活动或任务结束后非法滞留美国的实例。


________________

English Original

c. Recommendations and Alternatives to the Proposed 30-Day Departure Period Comments: Many commenters offered the alternative of keeping the 60-day departure period intact. Others recommended special departure period provisions for specific situations. For example, a commenter suggested maintaining a 60-day departure period for graduates of accredited health-professional programs and for students with timely-filed OPT/STEM OPT or cap-gap situations. Another commenter suggested limited, compliance-conditioned automatic grace extensions to reduce needless filings and “status gaps” for OPT/STEM OPT and R&D roles. One commenter suggested that the 60-day grace period be extended if the F-1 visa holder is seeking employment in the United States. Another commenter suggested graduates be granted a minimum 90-day grace period, extendable to 120 days when seeking academic employment. A commenter suggested maintaining the 60-day period while implementing additional reporting requirements in SEVIS at the end of the departure period or allowing more flexibility in departure periods based on individual circumstances. Commenters recommended that DHS focus on strengthening visa screening at the issuance stage rather than reducing the F-1 departure period to address security concerns. Rather than shortening the F-1 period, a commenter suggested extending the departure period for J-1 and M-1 visa holders to 60 days for consistency across student visa categories. Other commenters suggested making the departure period 45 days. Some commenters suggested requiring periodic SEVIS check-ins or improved reporting mechanisms, rather than shortening the grace period. Similarly, one commenter stated that to the extent the grace period reduction is intended to reduce the likelihood of potential unlawful stays or activities, a background check at the beginning of the approval process is the key. Three commenters recommended that the grace period be extended. One suggested extending it to a period of one year, rather than 30 days, another to a period of 180 days, and another that did not state a specific period of time. Another encouraged DHS to allow a middle-ground solution, such as fixed-term authorization combined with automatic grace periods, or tiered extensions based on academic progress and institutional standing. Response: DHS believes that changing the departure period for F nonimmigrants from 60 to 30 days is appropriate for several reasons as discussed in the NPRM. Under existing regulations, F-1 students are provided 60 days following the completion of their studies and any practical training to prepare for departure from the United States. However, this is twice as long as other SEVP programs (J and M nonimmigrants). In addition, this 60-day period is also six times longer than certain nonimmigrants who are authorized to remain in the United States for years but are only provided with a 10-day period to depart the United States. For example, DHS provides a 10-day period following the end of the alien's admission period as stated on the I-94 for aliens in the E-1, E-2, E-3, H-1B, L-1, and TN classifications in a 2016 rulemaking. DHS notes that a grace period of up to 10 days after the end of an authorized validity period provides a reasonable amount of time for such nonimmigrants to depart the United States or take other actions to extend, change, or otherwise maintain lawful status.[204] It is unclear to DHS why F students would need a significantly longer period of time to prepare for departure when other nonimmigrants have less time to prepare for departure. DHS believes that 30 days is sufficient and an appropriate balance between the 60-day and 10-day periods that aliens are granted to prepare for departure. As with the J and M nonimmigrants, F students are seeking admission to the United States to study at a U.S. educational institution. Thus, DHS believes the standard period of time of 30 days is reasonable and adequate period for F-1 students to prepare for departure and is in line with similar classifications (the M and J departure periods). DHS understands that there are legitimate reasons for F-1 nonimmigrants to require additional time to complete a program. As noted, the rule provides F-1 nonimmigrants with the opportunity to apply for EOS if required, or to depart and re-enter the United States. DHS disagrees that 60 days is insufficient to either apply for EOS if required, or to depart and re-enter the United States. 3. Practical Training: OPT, STEM OPT, CPT, and I-94 Expiration Dates Comments: A commenter requested clarification and examples as to how the fixed admission period and grace periods would interact with: (a) completion of Program A to post-completion OPT (and STEM OPT, if applicable) to enrollment in Program; (b) school processing timelines; and (c) any automatic or conditional extensions, if the rule is finalized. Response: The proposed rule provides timelines and mechanisms for maintaining status when transitioning between programs, engaging in post-completion OPT/STEM OPT, and applying for extensions. See new 8 CFR 214.2(f)(5), (f)(7), and (f)(8), and 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10). First, F-1 students are initially admitted for the length of their program as listed on Form I-20, not to exceed 4 years, plus a 30-day grace period for departure or to take steps to maintain lawful status. After completing Program A, a student may apply for post-completion OPT through their DSO and upon the DSO's recommendation, apply for EOS and employment authorization with USCIS. If EOS is approved for OPT, the student's period of admission is extended to the end date of the EAD for OPT, plus a 30-day grace period. If eligible, the student may apply for a STEM OPT extension. Both would require filing a subsequent EOS application and employment authorization to USCIS. If approved, the admission period extends to the new EAD end date for STEM OPT, plus a 30- day grace period. If the student wishes to begin a new program at a higher educational level, they must obtain a new Form I-20 and, if the new program will not be completed within the current admission period, apply for EOS before the end of their current authorized stay (including the 30-day grace period). However, if USCIS has not approved the EAD at the time of application for admission but the DSO has approved a term for OPT, the F or J nonimmigrant may be admitted for up to 240 days to allow for USCIS to adjudicate the EAD. The student must ensure their EOS application is filed before the current period of admission expires. If a student timely files an EOS application before their current admission period expires, they are considered in a period of authorized stay while the application is pending. See new 8 CFR 214.2(f)(5)(viii). For certain types of employment ( e.g., on-campus, CPT, severe economic hardship), employment authorization is automatically extended for up to 240 days while the EOS is pending. See new 8 CFR 214.2(f)(5)(viii) and new 8 CFR 274a.12(c)(3)(iii). If the EOS application is filed during the 30-day grace period after completion of studies or OPT, the student may continue studying but may not engage in practical training or employment until the EOS is approved. See new 8 CFR 214.2(f)(7)(iii)(B). The rule provides that USCIS may approve extensions in cases such as school closures, natural disasters, or other circumstances beyond the student's control, as described in the rule. See new 8 CFR 214.2(f)(7)(i)(C)( 2)( iii). Comments: Some commenters expressed concerns in that the rule would eliminate Day-1 CPT, indicating that fields such as business, computer science, and health sciences often require early practical training as part of the academic program. Along these lines, they stated that prohibiting Day-1 CPT would undermine the integrity of these academic programs and prevent students from meeting graduation requirements on time, while reducing the ability of U.S. industries to access talent trained in cutting-edge fields. Similarly, another commenter noted that the rule would impose financial accessibility and tuition burdens because students rely on Day-1 CPT to pay tuition and living expenses, given that foreign students must pay full tuition. The commenter called for DHS to retain flexibility for Day-1 CPT authorization, while addressing concerns through different methods, such as increase in reporting requirements or SEVIS oversight. Response: These comments misinterpret the rule as the rule does not prohibit or eliminate Day-1 CPT. The rule primarily imposes a fixed admission period of up to 4 years for F-1 students and requires students to apply for EOS if additional time is needed to complete their programs of study or for practical training. In doing so, it eliminates the D/S framework, but it does not make substantive changes to CPT. Separately, DHS notes that the primary purpose of the F-1 nonimmigrant status is for foreign students to study in the United States and not a pathway for employment. Moreover, as a requisite for the F-1 nonimmigrant visa, foreign students must provide evidence of financial ability to support themselves to study in the United States by demonstrating that they (or their sponsor) have sufficient funds to cover tuition and living expenses during the period of intended study. As such, F-1 students should not be relying on future or prospective income from Day-1 CPT as their primary means to financially support themselves in the United States. Comments: Many commenters stated the need for OPT reform, specifically highlighting OPT students working in STEM. One commenter said despite being backed by the INA, OPT is now used to bypass congressionally set limits on H-1B visas, with the program operating as an H-1B substitute. The commenter further states that this influx of OPT students into STEM fields has offset many American STEM graduates into other non-related fields. Another commenter suggested that, due to not being explicitly authorized by statute and disproportionally benefitting foreign graduates over U.S. citizens and permanent residents, the STEM OPT extension program should be phased out entirely. Response: This rule does not make any substantive changes to OPT and STEM OPT other than in the context of admission and extensions. Requests for OPT, STEM OPT, and H-1B reform are outside of the scope of this rule. However, DHS notes that while this rule does not make any substantive changes to OPT and STEM OPT other than in the context of admission period and extensions of stay, DHS believes that strengthening the F nonimmigration classification and creating more vetting mechanisms will help DHS in identifying issues of OPT and STEM OPT fraud and abuse. Comments: A commenter stated that the overlap between OPT processing and H-1B application cycles creates a 6-8 week window where status uncertainties could disrupt the talent pipeline. Response: DHS confirms that the rule does not modify or remove automatic extension of the authorized period of stay and employment authorization for F-1 students who are beneficiaries of timely filed H-1B cap-subject petitions. Eligible F-1 students will continue to be granted automatic extension of the authorized period of stay and employment authorization through April 1 of the relevant fiscal year or until the H-1B petition's validity start date, whichever is earlier. Comments: A commenter recommended clarification on the impact of the proposed rule in specific situations, including students who receive a Form I-20 for 3 years and a Form I-94 valid for 3 years, but graduate in 1.5 years. The commenter asked if these students need to apply for extension of their I-94 end stay when they apply for post-completion OPT, since the student would still be within their Form I-94 3-year timeframe. Alternatively, the commenter asked if these students wanted to apply for a STEM OPT, would they need to apply for a Form I-94 EOS before or concurrently with the STEM OPT extension. Response: In such cases, students will be able to continue pursuing their full course of study. If the I-20 says 3 years, their admission period on the I-94 will be 3 years plus the 30-day departure period. Before the I-94 expiration date, they will need to file for an EOS. They will also need to apply for EAD for OPT or STEM OPT simultaneously with the EOS. The EOS is only valid until the date of their employment authorization. Aliens who need additional time to complete their current course of study, including requests for post-completion OPT, STEM OPT, or academic training, or would like to start a new course of study or exchange visitor program must file for an EOS with USCIS. This EOS will be for an admission period up to the new program end date, or OPT end date, listed on the Form I-20 or DS-2019, or successor form, with the maximum period of 4-years. 4. OPT Regulatory Language Comments: A commenter stated that the proposed regulatory text at 8 CFR 214.2(f)(5)(i) only mentions OPT. The commenter noted that this may be an oversight and requested confirmation that transfers and change of level cases would also be mentioned in the regulations should the rule be finalized. Response: DHS agrees with the commenter and has updated the regulatory text to clarify a continuing F- 1 student may be granted additional time to complete their program of study as described in new 8 CFR 214.2(f)(7) and (f)(8)(iv), begin a new program of study, as described in new 8 CFR 214.2(f)(5)(ii)(D) and (f)(7), or following the completion of studies to engage in post-completion OPT and STEM OPT, as described in new 8 CFR 214.2(f)(5)(i)(D). 5. Leave of Absence Comments: Some commenters worried about instances where students might need a leave of absence, including needing to leave for medical, personal, or family reasons, or for mandatory military duty in the student's home country. Commenters sought clarification on how the rule would apply to students needing to take a leave of absence and return to resume their program and some expressed concern about the student needing to complete the EOS process with a possible denial. One commenter reported that, under the prior rules, students who needed this temporary leave of absence would depart the United States but could then return under a new SEVIS record at the same degree level to resume their studies. The commenter was unclear on the applicability of the new limitation relating to completion of a degree at the same educational level on this population. Response: An F-1 student may take a temporary absence of five months or less and return using his or her previously created SEVIS record and Form I-20, endorsed by the DSO for reentry. See8 CFR 214.2(f)(4), which is unchanged in this rulemaking. Prior to ceasing participation in a full course of study, the student must inform his or her DSO and the DSO must terminate the student's SEVIS record for the reason of Authorized Early Withdrawal. Prior to the student seeking reentry, the DSO must submit a correction request in SEVIS to return the student's SEVIS record back to active status. The SEVIS record must be in Active status prior to the student requesting reentry into the United States. A DSO can submit a request to change an F-1 student's SEVIS record to Active up to 60 days in advance of the student's return from a temporary absence. If an F-1 student is absent from the United States for more than five months, except in cases where a student is studying abroad, the student will need to seek admission in initial status, which includes but is not limited to, obtaining a new Form I-20 and will result in a new determination of the student's authorized period of stay. Further, this final rule provides that if the student is unable to complete his or her program of study by the end date indicated on his or her Form I-20 due to compelling academic reasons, documented illness or medical condition and/or circumstances beyond the student's control, the student may apply for an EOS from USCIS. See new 8 CFR 214.2(f)(7). The student should carefully consider the timing of his or her extension request but in any case, USCIS must receive the request for extension prior to the program end date noted on the Form I-20.

中文翻译

本节汇总了公众针对提案提出的关于保持 60 天离境期的替代建议、关于实际培训（OPT/STEM OPT/CPT）、请假（Leave of Absence）等方面的意见，以及国土安全部（DHS）的官方回应：


1. 针对 30 天离境期的替代建议与 DHS 回应：


* 公众建议： 许多评论者建议保留 60 天离境期，或者针对健康专业毕业生、OPT/STEM OPT 申请人延长至 90 至 120 天，或通过加强海外签证筛选来代替缩短离境期。


* DHS 回应： DHS 认为 30 天完全足够且合理。相较于 M 类和 J 类（30 天）以及许多获准停留多年但离境期仅有 10 天的其他非移民类别（如 H-1B、L-1 等），给 F-1 学生 30 天的准备时间已经兼顾了公平与合理。


2. 实际培训（OPT、STEM OPT、CPT）与 Day-1 CPT：


* CPT 与 Day-1 CPT： 评论者担心该规则会废除 Day-1 CPT。DHS 澄清：该规则并未禁止或废除 Day-1 CPT，它只是取消了 D/S 框架并设定最长 4 年的固定准入期，未对 CPT 做实质性修改。同时重申 F-1 并非就业途径，学生不应依赖 Day-1 CPT 收入作为主要生活来源。


* OPT/STEM OPT 与 H-1B Cap-Gap： 规则未对 OPT、STEM OPT 实体政策或 H-1B Cap-Gap 自动延期做出实质性修改。符合条件的学生在申请 OPT、STEM OPT 或等待 H-1B 审理期间依然能享受自动延期保护。如果 I-94 到期前项目提前完成，学生需要通过提交 EOS 来衔接 OPT。


3. 请假（Leave of Absence）：


* 公众关切： 询问因医疗、个人、家庭原因或母国兵役需要请假并返回原项目的学生将如何受新规影响。


* DHS 回应： 5 个月或以下的临时离校（Leave of Absence）规则保持不变。学生可按现行规定（通过 DSO 授权提前退学并在回归前恢复 SEVIS 激活）离境并返回。若离校超过 5 个月，则需以初始状态重新获取 I-20 并重新确定准入期。若因学术原因、疾病或不可控因素无法按时毕业，则需向 USCIS 申请 EOS。


详细翻译


c. 对拟议 30 天离境期的建议与替代方案


意见： 许多评论者提出了保持 60 天离境期不变的替代方案。其他人则针对特定情况推荐了特殊的离境期条款。例如，一位评论者建议为获得认证的健康专业项目毕业生以及处于按时提交的 OPT/STEM OPT 或名额缝隙（Cap-Gap）情况下的学生维持 60 天的离境期。另一位评论者建议实行受合规条件限制的有限自动宽限期延期，以减少针对 OPT/STEM OPT 和研发角色的不必要申报和“状态间隙”。


一位评论者建议，如果 F-1 签证持有者正在美国寻求就业，应将 60 天的宽限期延长。另一位评论者建议毕业生在寻求学术就业时获得最短 90 天的宽限期，可延长至 120 天。一位评论者建议在离境期结束时在 SEVIS 中实施额外的报告要求的同时维持 60 天的期限，或者根据个人情况在离境期内提供更多灵活性。评论者建议 DHS 重点在签发阶段加强签证筛查，而不是缩短 F-1 离境期来解决安全问题。


为了替代缩短 F-1 期限，一位评论者建议将 J-1 和 M-1 签证持有者的离境期延长至 60 天，以确保学生签证类别之间的一致性。其他评论者建议将离境期定为 45 天。一些评论者建议要求定期的 SEVIS 签到或改进报告机制，而不是缩短宽限期。同样，一位评论者表示，如果缩短宽限期的目的是为了减少潜在非法滞留或活动的可能，那么在审批流程开始时进行背景调查才是关键。


三位评论者建议延长宽限期。一位建议延长至一年，而不是 30 天；另一位建议延长至 180 天；还有一位没有说明具体时间段。另一位鼓励 DHS 允许折中方案，例如固定期限授权与自动宽限期相结合，或者基于学术进展和机构地位的分级延期。


回应： DHS 认为将 F 类非移民的离境期从 60 天缩短至 30 天是合适的，原因正如 NPRM 中所讨论的那样。根据现行法规，F-1 学生在完成学业和任何实际培训后有 60 天的时间来准备离开美国。然而，这比其他 SEVP 项目（J 和 M 类非移民）的时间长一倍。此外，这个 60 天的期限也比某些获准在美国停留多年但仅被给予 10 天时间离境的非移民长六倍。例如，DHS 在 2016 年的一项规则制定中规定，对于 E-1、E-2、E-3、H-1B、L-1 和 TN 类别的外籍人员，在其 I-94 上规定的入境期限结束后的离境期为 10 天。DHS 指出，在授权有效期结束后的最多 10 天宽限期内，为此类非移民提供了一段合理的时间来离开美国或采取其他行动以延长、更改或以其他方式维持合法身份 [204]。DHS 并不清楚为什么在其他非移民准备离境的时间更少的情况下，F 类学生会需要显著更长的时间来准备离境。


DHS 认为 30 天是充足的，并且是在外籍人员获准准备离境的 60 天和 10 天之间的一个适当平衡。与 M 和 J 类非移民一样，F 类学生寻求进入美国是为了在美教育机构学习。因此，DHS 认为 30 天的标准时间段对于 F-1 学生准备离境来说是合理且充足的，并且符合类似类别（M 和 J 类的离境期）。DHS 理解 F-1 类非移民有合法的理由需要额外的时间来完成项目。如前所述，该规则为 F-1 类非移民提供了在需要时申请 EOS 的机会，或者离境并重新进入美国。DHS 不同意 60 天不足以申请 EOS 或离境并重新进入美国的说法。


3. 实际培训：OPT、STEM OPT、CPT 和 I-94 到期日


意见： 一位评论者要求澄清并举例说明固定入境期限和宽限期将如何与以下情况进行互动：(a) 从项目 A 完成到毕业后 OPT（以及适用时的 STEM OPT）再到注册项目；(b) 学校处理时间线；以及 (c) 如果规则最终敲定，任何自动或有条件延期。


回应： 拟议规则提供了在项目之间过渡、参与毕业后 OPT/STEM OPT 以及申请延期时维持身份的时间线和机制。参阅新 8 CFR 214.2(f)(5)、(f)(7) 和 (f)(8)，以及 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)。首先，F-1 学生最初准入的期限为其 Form I-20 上列出的项目长度，最长不超过 4 年，外加用于离境或采取步骤维持合法身份的 30 天宽限期。在完成项目 A 后，学生可以通过其 DSO 申请毕业后 OPT，并在 DSO 的推荐下，向 USCIS 申请 EOS 和工作授权。如果 OPT 的 EOS 获批，学生的入境期限将延长至 OPT 的 EAD 卡到期日，外加 30 天宽限期。如果符合资格，学生可以申请 STEM OPT 延期。这两者都需要向 USCIS 提交后续的 EOS 申请和工作授权。如果获批，入境期限将延长至 STEM OPT 的新 EAD 到期日，外加 30 天宽限期。如果学生希望在更高的教育阶段开始一个新项目，他们必须获得一笔新的 Form I-20，并且如果新项目无法在当前入境期限内完成，则必须在当前授权停留期（包括 30 天宽限期）结束前申请 EOS。然而，如果在申请入境时 USCIS 尚未批准 EAD，但 DSO 已经批准了 OPT 的学期，F 或 J 类非移民可以获准入境最长 240 天，以便 USCIS 裁决 EAD。


学生必须确保在当前入境期限到期前提交 EOS 申请。如果学生在当前入境期限到期前及时提交了 EOS 申请，则在申请审理期间他们被视为处于授权停留期。参阅新 8 CFR 214.2(f)(5)(viii)。对于某些类型的就业（例如校内就业、CPT、严重经济困难），在 EOS 审理期间，工作授权会自动延长最多 240 天。参阅新 8 CFR 214.2(f)(5)(viii) 和新 8 CFR 274a.12(c)(3)(iii)。如果在完成学业或 OPT 之后的 30 天宽限期内提交 EOS 申请，学生可以继续学习，但在 EOS 获批之前不得从事实际培训或就业。参阅新 8 CFR 214.2(f)(7)(iii)(B)。该规则规定，USCIS 可以在学校关闭、自然灾害或规则中所述的其他超出学生控制范围的情况下列情况下批准延期。参阅新 8 CFR 214.2(f)(7)(i)(C)(2)(iii)。


意见： 一些评论者担心该规则将取消 Day-1 CPT，并指出商业、计算机科学和健康科学等领域通常需要将早期的实际培训作为学术项目的一部分。顺着这些思路，他们表示禁止 Day-1 CPT 将破坏这些学术项目的完整性，并阻碍学生按时达到毕业要求，同时降低美国工业获取在尖端领域接受培训的人才的能力。同样，另一位评论者指出，该规则将强加财务可及性和学费负担，因为由于外国学生必须支付全额学费，学生依靠 Day-1 CPT 来支付学费和生活费。该评论者呼吁 DHS 保留对 Day-1 CPT 授权的灵活性，同时通过其他方法解决关切，例如增加报告要求或 SEVIS 监督。


回应： 这些评论误解了该规则，因为该规则并未禁止或取消 Day-1 CPT。该规则主要为 F-1 学生强加了最长 4 年的固定入境期限，并要求学生在需要额外时间来完成其学习项目或进行实际培训时申请 EOS。在这样做时，它取消了 D/S 框架，但并未对 CPT 作出实质性修改。


另外，DHS 指出，F-1 非移民身份的主要目的是供外国学生在美国学习，而不是就业的途径。此外，作为 F-1 非移民签证的必要条件，外国学生必须提供自费在美学习的财务能力证明，即证明他们（或其赞助方）在预期的学习期间拥有足够的资金来支付学费和生活费。因此，F-1 学生不应将来自 Day-1 CPT 的未来或预期收入作为其在美国经济上支持自己的主要手段。


意见： 许多评论者表示需要对 OPT 进行改革，特别强调在 STEM 领域工作的 OPT 学生。一位评论者说，尽管有 INA 的支持，但 OPT 现在被用来绕过国会设定的 H-1B 签证限制，该项目作为 H-1B 的替代品运作。该评论者进一步表示，这批 OPT 学生涌入 STEM 领域，抵消了许多美国 STEM 毕业生进入其他非相关领域的努力。另一位评论者建议，由于没有得到法律的明确授权，且不成比例地使外国毕业生受益于美国公民和永久居民，STEM OPT 延期项目应该完全逐步取消。


回应： 除了在入境期限和延期的上下文中之外，本规则未对 OPT 和 STEM OPT 作出任何实质性修改。对 OPT、STEM OPT 和 H-1B 改革的请求超出了本规则的范围。然而，DHS 指出，虽然本规则除入境期限和停留延期上下文外未对 OPT 和 STEM OPT 做任何实质性修改，但 DHS 相信，加强 F 类非移民身份并建立更多审查机制将有助于 DHS 识别 OPT 和 STEM OPT 欺诈和滥用问题。


意见： 一位评论者指出，OPT 审理与 H-1B 申请周期之间的重叠产生了一个为期 6-8 周的窗口期，在此期间状态的不确定性可能会破坏人才管道。


回应： DHS 确认该规则并未修改或取消对作为及时提交的 H-1B 名额附带呈请受益人的 F-1 学生授权停留期和工作授权的自动延长。符合条件的 F-1 学生将继续获准自动延长授权停留期和工作授权，直至相关财政年度的 4 月 1 日，或直至 H-1B 呈请的效力开始日期，以较早者为准。


意见： 一位评论者建议澄清拟议规则在特定情况下的影响，包括收到 3 年期 Form I-20 且 Form I-94 有效期为 3 年、但在 1.5 年内毕业的学生。该评论者询问，当这些学生申请毕业后 OPT 时，是否需要申请延长其 I-94 结束停留期，因为学生仍在其 Form I-94 的 3 年时间框架内。或者，评论者询问，如果这些学生想要申请 STEM OPT，他们是否需要在中国 STEM OPT 延期之前或同时申请 Form I-94 EOS。


回应： 在此类情况下，学生将能够继续修读其全日制课程。如果 I-20 上写的是 3 年，他们在 I-94 上的入境期限将是 3 年外加 30 天离境期。在 I-94 到期日之前，他们将需要提交 EOS 申请。他们还需在提交 EOS 的同时，为 OPT 或 STEM OPT 申请 EAD 卡。EOS 仅在他们的工作授权日期之前有效。需要额外时间来完成当前课程（包括申请毕业后 OPT、STEM OPT 或学术培训），或者想要开始新课程或交流访问项目的外国国民，必须向 USCIS 申请 EOS。此 EOS 的入境期限最长为 Form I-20 或 DS-2019 或后续表格上列出的新项目结束日期或 OPT 结束日期，最长期限为 4 年。


4. OPT 法规文本


意见： 一位评论者指出，8 CFR 214.2(f)(5)(i) 中提出的法规文本仅提及 OPT。评论者注意到这可能是一个疏忽，并请求确认如果规则最终敲定，转学和学历级别变更也将提及于法规中。


回应： DHS 同意评论者的意见，并已更新了法规文本，以澄清可以授予在读 F-1 学生额外时间来完成其项目（如新 8 CFR 214.2(f)(7) 和 (f)(8)(iv) 中所述）、开始新项目（如新 8 CFR 214.2(f)(5)(ii)(D) 和 (f)(7) 中所述）、或在完成学业后参与毕业后 OPT 和 STEM OPT（如新 8 CFR 214.2(f)(5)(i)(D) 中所述）。


5. 请假（Leave of Absence）


意见： 一些评论者担心学生可能需要请假的情况，包括因医疗、个人或家庭原因需要离开，或因母国强制性兵役需要离开。评论者寻求澄清该规则将如何适用于需要请假并返回以恢复项目的学生，一些人对学生可能需要完成 EOS 过程且可能面临被拒表达了担忧。一位评论者报告称，在先前的规则下，需要这种临时请假的学生会离开美国，但随后可以以相同的学位级别在新的 SEVIS 记录下返回以恢复学习。评论者不清楚关于在同一教育级别完成学位的限制对这一人群的适用性。


回应： F-1 学生可以进行 5 个月或更短的临时离开，并使用其先前创建的 SEVIS 记录和 Form I-20（经 DSO 背书用于重新入境）返回。参阅 8 CFR 214.2(f)(4)，该条款在本规则制定中未作修改。在停止参与全日制课程之前，学生必须通知其 DSO，并且 DSO 必须以“授权提前退学（Authorized Early Withdrawal）”为由终止学生的 SEVIS record。在学生寻求重新入境之前，DSO 必须在 SEVIS 中提交更正请求，将学生的 SEVIS 记录恢复到活跃状态。在学生请求重新入境美国之前，SEVIS 记录必须处于活跃（Active）状态。DSO 可以提交请求，在学生从临时离开中返回之前最多提前 60 天将 F-1 学生的 SEVIS 记录更改为 Active。如果 F-1 学生离开美国超过 5 个月（在海外留学的学生情况除外），学生将需要以初始状态寻求入境，这包括但不限于获得新的 Form I-20，并将导致对学生授权停留期的新确定。


此外，本最终规则规定，如果由于令人信服的学术原因、有记录的疾病或医疗状况和/或超出学生控制范围的情况，学生无法在 Form I-20 上指示的结束日期前完成其学习项目，学生可以向 USCIS 申请 EOS。参阅新 8 CFR 214.2(f)(7)。学生应仔细考虑其延期请求的时机，但在任何情况下，USCIS 必须在 Form I-20 上注明的项目结束日期之前收到延期请求。


________________

English Original

K. J Nonimmigrants 1. General Impacts on all J-1 Programs Comments: Commenters expressed significant concerns about the rule's impact on J-1 nonimmigrants, their dependents, and on the exchange program categories. Commenters cautioned that the rigidity of the rule could threaten institutional knowledge and research outcomes in various industries, including public health, medical treatment, and scientific advancement more broadly, as they rely on experienced, stable research personnel and staff in J-1 status. Relatedly, a commenter warned about the loss of federal return on investment, explaining that many research projects are funded by federal agencies, such as the NSF and NIH, and such projects could be interrupted by the proposed rule, directly threatening the integrity of publicly funded research and the return on taxpayer investment. Commenters stated that the rule imposes time limitations on J-1 categories and requires exchange visitors to undergo a delayed and costly extension process through USCIS, removing the flexibility currently inherent in the exchange visitor program and resulting in financial and administrative burdens to sponsors, students, host families, and schools. Similarly, another commenter further noted that the new costs and processing requirements would impose a disproportionate financial burden and hinder a sponsor's ability to manage extensions efficiently under existing DoS regulations. Specifically, commenters warned that EOS delays or denials could render extension decisions ineffective, create legal uncertainty, interrupt students' ability to remain enrolled, disrupt research, healthcare systems, educational progression, and discourage participation in U.S. programs thereby reducing overall participation in J-1 programs. Some noted that these programs often require more than four years to complete,[205] meaning multiple EOS applications would be necessary. An NGO reported that USCIS already has a record backlog of more than 11 million pending cases and a processing time of up to 18 months; therefore, USCIS will not be able to successfully manage the additional filings through the elimination of the DoS process. The NGO noted this would greatly impact the exchange model by no longer being able to support J-1 students' goal of extending their stay, fulfilling their academic aspirations, or submitting a dual application with the intention to study at two different universities for a year-long exchange experience. In addition, commenters voiced concerns relating to SEVIS and its administrative challenges. Specifically, a commenter stated that the proposed rule would create confusion in the system upon which sponsors and government officials rely for tracking J-1 program details. The commenter stated that currently, each exchange visitor has a single end date tracked through SEVIS, but with the proposed changes, the J-1 nonimmigrants would have a DS-2019 end date, and a Form I-94 end date, which might be different. The commenter also noted that this may cause a dependent family member to have a Form I-94 record with yet another end date, making it more challenging for J programs to track the status of these nonimmigrants, which could potentially lead to inadvertent lapses in compliance. Similarly, another commenter warned that an EOS application would not provide a meaningful additional checkpoint and might even weaken the existing provisions in SEVIS by giving program participants unneeded and unmonitored time after program completion. Response: DHS appreciates these concerns about all the changes that will have to be made to comply with this final rule. DHS recognizes this rule may result in adjustments by stakeholders, administrative costs, and time, but the enhanced national security and program integrity outweigh these burdens. SEVIS is being updated to handle these changes. For example, the I-94 date will now be visible in SEVIS so that DSOs and ROs will be able to flag the need for an EOS and inform the participants. However, dependents will be expected to track their own need for an EOS if, for some reason, the dates on the principal's I-94 does not match that of the dependents. The I-94s will always include the 30-day departure period afforded F and J nonimmigrants; therefore, there should be no new unmonitored time that hasn't existed previously. If the principals finish their programs early, SEVIS will be updated to reflect that change and the family will have 30 days to depart after the completion of the program. The D/S framework generally lacks predetermined points in time for USCIS or CBP immigration officers to directly evaluate whether F, J, and I nonimmigrants are maintaining their status and poses a challenge to DHS's ability to effectively monitor and oversee these categories of nonimmigrants. DHS believes that 4-year maximum admission periods will provide more frequent direct check-in points for nonimmigrants and align the educational structure of the programs for their intended purposes. In sum, DHS believes these measures are necessary to protect the integrity of these nonimmigrant programs due to heightened concerns related to fraud, abuse, and national security. In addition, DHS does not believe that the rule will cause educational or employment disruptions for exchange visitors, schools, sponsors, or any other stakeholders because the rule allows J-1 nonimmigrants to continue employment and training activities, consistent with the exchange program, while a properly filed extension request is pending, for up to 240 days beyond a J-1 nonimmigrant's expiration of the authorized period of admission. DHS notes that the 240-day rule is the same requirement for most other nonimmigrants, including members of many other professions ( e.g., H-1B, L-1, O-1, etc.). DHS also notes that USCIS generally recommends that nonimmigrants apply for EOS within the six months [206] preceding the expiration of their current stay and the 240 only starts tolling after the expiration of their current stay. DHS acknowledges that a J-1 nonimmigrant will not be able to continue employment if the EOS application is still pending after 240 days. However, the J-1 nonimmigrant will be able to remain in the United States in a period of authorized stay while the EOS request remains pending. While this could result in administrative costs and time to adjust for changes, DHS believes the enhanced national security and program integrity outweigh these burdens. Furthermore, as discussed previously, J-1 nonimmigrants may be able to seek expedited adjudication through USCIS in certain instances.[207] Given these options, DHS disagrees that a fixed period of admission and the requirement to file an EOS application is unduly burdensome such that this rule will discourage J-1 exchange visitors from participating in these programs. 2. Duplicative Processes and Regulatory Authority Conflicts Comments: Multiple commenters expressed concern that the proposed rule would create a duplicative process for J-1 exchange visitors seeking extensions, writing that DoS already has effective processes in place for J-1 extensions. The commenters stated that DoS currently has sole authority when it comes to approving program extensions for all J-1 program categories and the proposed rule, if finalized, would complicate this authority by requiring participants to file a separate EOS application with USCIS. A commenter requested clarification about how the DoS will coordinate with DHS to minimize contradictory timelines between visa issuance, I-20/DS-2019 validity, and EOS approval. Commenters stated that the existing D/S framework for J-1 nonimmigrants includes strict limitations, making this rule unnecessary. On that same note, commenters emphasized that J-1 exchange visitors are carefully monitored, with DSOs and ROs regularly reporting information including address changes, academic progress, and current employment details to DHS and to DoS. A commenter also noted that J-1 programs currently have defined maximum program lengths, category change restrictions, and additional safeguards such as the 2-year home residency requirement and repeat participation bars that effectively discourage extended stays. A DSO noted that DoS regulations are sufficient to accomplish DHS's goals, as the J-1 program contains 14 different categories of which many already have regulatory time limits in place under 22 CFR 62.20(i)(3), rendering this rule duplicative and unnecessary. Response: DHS disagrees with the commenter that this rule creates duplicative processes that are not necessary. Notably, the DoS regulations for J-1 nonimmigrants to extend their programs are distinct from the requirements for USCIS to adjudicate their EOS. The program period refers to the official duration of the academic or training program in which the nonimmigrant is enrolled ( e.g., the start and end dates of a degree or course); the period of stay is the length of time the nonimmigrant is authorized to remain in the United States, which may include time before, during, and after the program period, as permitted by their visa status. While an RO's recommendation and DoS approval of program extension may be one factor in favor of an EOS, an immigration officer also reviews factors relating to an alien's immigration status, including any criminal records and grounds of removability that may be triggered based on criminal background checks. ROs are not immigration officers and lack the statutory and regulatory authority to grant EOS. The EOS process under this rule would give immigration officers a mechanism to make this evaluation at reasonably frequent intervals. DHS expects this change to deter and prevent fraud. To begin the EOS process, a J-1 nonimmigrant will first have to obtain a recommendation from ROs, and approval from DoS where applicable, under the DoS regulations. DHS has specified in the rule that the EOS may be granted subject to the DoS regulations on the extension of the program, contingent upon DoS' approval of the extension of the program itself. See new 8 CFR 214.2(j)(1)(iv)(C) (“[s]ubject to the restrictions in the regulations at 22 CFR part 62, extensions of stay may be granted for a period up to the length of the program, as listed on the Form DS-2019, or successor form, not to exceed 4-years, unless the J-1 exchange visitor is otherwise restricted by regulations at 22 CFR part 62.”). Specifically, DoS regulations provide that the ROs may extend a J-1 exchange visitor's participation in the Exchange Visitor Program up to the limit of the permissible period of participation authorized for the alien's specific program category. See22 CFR 62.43. The RO then issues a duly executed Form DS-2019 reflecting the extension and provides a notification copy of the form to the DoS. See22 CFR 62.43(a)-(b). Extensions beyond the maximum program duration are allowed in some program categories for exceptional or unusual circumstances, with approval from DoS. To obtain approval for such extensions, the RO must submit an electronic request that justifies the petition and provides supporting documentation to the DoS on behalf of the participant. See22 CFR 62.43(c). Thereafter, under the requirements of this new rule, a J-1 nonimmigrant would need to apply for EOS with USCIS. If DoS denies the extension of the exchange visitor program, the nonimmigrant has no basis on which to file an EOS with USCIS. USCIS would deny the petition because there is no valid underlying program. In sum, DoS and DHS processes and systems are necessarily different due to the unique responsibilities of the two departments. The processes established in this rule provide DHS with mechanisms to deter and prevent fraud, and therefore, are necessary to implement. Under the D/S framework, there are no background checks required unless the nonimmigrant applies for a change of status to a different nonimmigrant status, or another benefit with USCIS. This has created situations where nonimmigrants in D/S can be present for long periods in the United States without undergoing U.S. government review in the form of an EOS application, including accompanying background checks, or subsequent visa application or inspection at a POE, because program extensions under the D/S framework can be acquired through application to a DSO or RO. Recognizing and addressing this vulnerability is a key part of this rule's national security goals and is vital to increase the U.S. government's oversight and monitoring of these aliens. 3. Impacts to U.S. Foreign Relations and Intellectual Exchange Comments: Commenters stated that a “one-size-fits-all” admission period limit could deter nonimmigrants from participating in cultural and educational exchange programs. Commenters stated the proposed rule would negatively affect U.S. interests and reduce exchange programs that build goodwill and mutual understanding. Commenters asserted that the proposed changes to the J-1 program conflict with the United States' objective of strengthening its economic and intellectual capital by discouraging international participation and by imposing artificial limits on scholars' stays, signaling a retreat from the nation's commitment to mutual understanding and knowledge sharing. A commenter recommended that DHS reconsider the proposed modification to the J-1 visa provisions and maintain the recent policy changes that have supported the program's success. The commenter explained that historically the J-1 visa has facilitated cultural and educational exchange, and that the removal of the 2-year home-country physical-presence requirement for J-1 visa holders from 34 countries, including China, India, and South Korea, represented a significant step toward strengthening international collaboration and improved the United States' ability to retain global talent. The commenter wrote that reinstating or altering this requirement could deter prospective exchange visitors, particularly those from countries recently removed from the DoS Skills List and further asserted that such changes could reduce the competitiveness of U.S. institutions in attracting top-tier international scholars and researchers. Response: DHS appreciates these concerns. However, DHS does not intend for the rule to result in a significant decline in foreign student participation in J-1 programs nor a significant reduction in talent, technological innovation, or economic benefits associated with foreign nationals. DHS asserts that the United States will continue to attract talented foreign exchange students, scholars, researchers, and physicians because U.S. institutions offer a high-quality education. For further discussion on this issue regarding participation in J-1 programs, see General Impacts on all J-1 Programs, Sec. IV.K.1 above. DHS also notes that the proposed rule changes how J-1s are admitted and how they extend their stay, but it does not affect the two-year home residency requirement. If a J-1 is subject to INA 212(e), they must still fulfill or obtain a waiver for that requirement before certain immigration benefits are available, regardless of the new admission procedures under this rule. 4. Grace Period for J-1 Nonimmigrant Classification Comments: A couple of commenters stated that the J-1 grace periods should be maintained or extended. Furthermore, a commenter expressed that the J-1 grace period should be increased to 60 days. Another commenter expressed concern about the applicability and eligibility requirements of the 30-day grace period under the proposed rule, and requested clarification on what would happen to J-1 nonimmigrants who do not complete their academic objectives. Response: DHS has considered the effects of the rule on J-1 nonimmigrant physicians, teachers, and other exchange visitor program categories. DHS does not believe that this rule will discourage J-1 exchange visitors from participating in exchange programs. DHS disagrees that the 30-day grace period is an insufficient grace period for J-1 nonimmigrants and declines to increase the period to 60 days. In fact, the regulations prior to this rule provided a 30-day period for departure and this rule does not change that. See8 CFR 214.2(j)(1)(ii) (2025). J-1 nonimmigrants who are unable to complete their programs on time and need additional time to complete their academic objectives may file for EOS to USCIS, which will adjudicate the EOS. In addition, DHS notes that as long as J-1 nonimmigrants submit an EOS prior to the expiration of the I-94, which includes the 30-day grace period, their extension request will be considered timely filed, and the J-1 nonimmigrants will be able to continue their J-1 authorized activities in accordance with the requirements outlined in new 8 CFR 214.2(j)(vii). Allowed activities are also discussed in section IV.K.1 above. 5. J-2 Dependents Comments: Commenters expressed concern regarding the requirement that J-2 dependents also need to apply for an extension. A commenter stated that the proposed rule is not clear on how the admission of J-1 dependents will be impacted if this rule is finalized. The commenter requested clarification about whether dependent admissions are automatically tied to the approval of the principal's visa approval. Response: The authorized period of initial admission for a J-2 spouse and unmarried children under the age of 21 cannot exceed the period of authorized admission of the principal J-1 exchange visitor. Likewise, EOS for J-2 dependents cannot exceed the authorized admission period of the principal J-1 exchange visitor. See new 8 CFR 214.2(j)(1)(ii)(B) and new 8 CFR 214.2(j)(1)(iv)(E). A J-2 dependent's EOS application may either be included on the J-1 nonimmigrant's EOS application, or the J-2 nonimmigrants must file their own EOS. On the application for EOS, the J-2 nonimmigrants must prove that they are maintaining lawful status and have not engaged in unauthorized employment. Furthermore, if the J-1 nonimmigrant's EOS request is denied, the J-2 nonimmigrants' EOS will also be denied.

中文翻译

本节汇总了公众针对拟议规则中对 J 类（交流访问学者）项目的影响、与国务院（DoS）职权的潜在冲突、国际关系与智力交流、宽限期以及 J-2 家属提出的质疑，以及国土安全部（DHS）的官方回应：


1. J-1 项目的普遍影响与行政负担：


* 公众关切： 评论者担心 4 年期限限制和 USCIS 繁琐的延期流程会威胁公共卫生、医学治疗和科学研究领域的稳定性，中断 NSF 和 NIH 等联邦资助的项目。面对堆积如山的 USCIS 积压案件，USCIS 无法高效处理。此外，SEVIS 系统中 I-94 到期日与 DS-2019 到期日可能不一致，增加了合规难度。


* DHS 回应： DHS 承认调整成本，但强调增强国家安全和项目完整性更为重要。SEVIS 系统已进行更新以显示 I-94 日期。只要正确提交 EOS，J-1 持有者在审理期间享有最长 240 天的工作和培训连续性授权。


2. 职责冲突与重复审批：


* 公众关切： 批评者指出 DoS 已经具备完善的 J-1 延期机制（包含 14 个类别的严格时间限制和 RO 监督），要求通过 USCIS 另外进行 EOS 属于不必要的重复审批。


* DHS 回应： DHS 澄清两者的性质不同：DoS 批准的是“学术或项目期限”，而 USCIS 审批的是“在美合法停留身份（Period of Stay）”。RO 并无执法权，而 EOS 审核能提供必要的背景调查和防范欺诈机制。若 DoS 拒绝项目延期，USCIS 将直接拒绝 EOS。


3. 对外关系与智力交流：


* 公众关切： “一刀切”的期限限制会削弱美国吸引顶尖全球人才和推动国际合作的能力。


* DHS 回应： 美国的高质量教育和科研资源依然具备强大吸引力。该规则不影响现有的两年回国服务期（INA 212(e)）要求。


4. J-1 宽限期与 J-2 家属：


* J-1 宽限期： 部分人建议将 J-1 离境宽限期延长至 60 天。DHS 维持现行规定，即 30 天离境宽限期保持不变，未完成目标者可提前提交 EOS。


* J-2 家属： J-2 家属的停留期受限于 J-1 主申请人。J-2 可随同主申请人一起提交 EOS 或单独提交，若主申请人被拒，J-2 的延期申请也会被拒绝。


详细翻译


K. J 类非移民


1. 对所有 J-1 项目的普遍影响


意见： 评论者对该规则对 J-1 非移民、其家属以及交流项目类别的影响表达了严重关切。评论者警告说，该规则的僵化性可能会威胁到各个行业（包括公共卫生、医学治疗和更广泛的科学进步）的机构知识和研究成果，因为这些行业依赖于 J-1 身份的经验丰富、稳定的研究人员和员工。相关地，一位评论者警告说联邦投资回报率（ROI）的损失，解释说许多研究项目由联邦机构（如 NSF 和 NIH）资助，此类项目可能会被拟议规则中断，直接威胁到公共资助研究的完整性和纳税人投资的回报。评论者表示，该规则对 J-1 类别施加了时间限制，并要求交流访问者通过 USCIS 经历一个延迟且代价高昂的延期过程，剥夺了交流访问项目原本固有的灵活性，并给赞助方、学生、寄宿家庭和学校带来了财务和行政负担。同样，另一位评论者进一步指出，新的成本和审理要求将强加不成比例的财务负担，并阻碍赞助方根据现行 DoS 法规高效管理延期的能力。


具体而言，评论者警告说，EOS 延误或拒绝可能会使延期决定失效、产生法律不确定性、中断学生继续注册的能力、扰乱研究、医疗系统、教育进展，并阻碍对美国项目的参与，从而减少对 J-1 项目的整体参与。一些人指出，这些项目通常需要四年以上的时间才能完成 [205]，这意味着需要进行多次 EOS 申请。一个非政府组织（NGO）报告称，USCIS 已经拥有超过 1100 万待决案件的创纪录积压，审理时间长达 18 个月；因此，USCIS 将无法通过取消 DoS 流程来成功管理额外的申报。该 NGO 指出，这将极大冲击交流模式，因为不再能够支持 J-1 学生延长停留、实现学术抱负或提交双重申请以在一年的交流体验中在两所不同大学学习的目标。


此外，评论者表达了与 SEVIS 及其行政挑战相关的关切。具体而言，一位评论者表示，拟议规则将在赞助商和政府官员依赖用于追踪 J-1 项目详情的系统中造成混淆。评论者表示，目前每个交流访问者都有通过 SEVIS 追踪的单一结束日期，但在做出拟议更改后，J-1 非移民将拥有一个 DS-2019 结束日期和一个 Form I-94 结束日期，这两者可能不同。评论者还指出，这可能导致受抚养的家属拥有一个带有又一个结束日期的 Form I-94 记录，这使得 J 项目追踪这些非移民的状态更具挑战性，并可能导致无意中违反合规。同样，另一位评论者警告说，EOS 申请不会提供有意义的额外检查点，甚至可能通过在项目完成后给项目参与者提供不需要且不受监督的时间，来削弱 SEVIS 中的现有条款。


回应： DHS 感谢对遵守本最终规则必须进行的所有更改的这些关切。DHS 认识到此规则可能会导致利益相关者进行调整、产生行政成本和时间，但增强的国家安全和项目完整性超过了这些负担。SEVIS 正在更新以处理这些更改。例如，I-94 日期现在将在 SEVIS 中可见，以便 DSO 和 RO 能够标记对 EOS 的需求并通知参与者。然而，如果由于某种原因，主申请人的 I-94 上的日期与家属的不匹配，家属仍将被期望追踪自己对 EOS 的需求。I-94 将始终包含 F 和 J 类非移民享有的 30 天离境期；因此，不应存在以前不存在的新不受监督时间。如果主申请人提前完成项目，SEVIS 将进行更新以反映该更改，并且家庭将在项目完成后有 30 天的时间离境。


D/S 框架通常缺乏预定时间点供 USCIS 或 CBP 移民官员直接评估 F、J 和 I 类非移民是否在维持其身份，并对 DHS 有效监测和监督这些非移民类别的能力构成了挑战。DHS 认为，最长 4 年的准入期将为非移民提供更频繁的直接报到点，并使项目的教育结构与其预定目的保持一致。总之，DHS 认为，由于对欺诈、滥用和国家安全的担忧加剧，这些措施对于保护这些非移民项目的完整性是必要的。


此外，DHS 不认为该规则会对交流访问者、学校、赞助方或任何其他利益相关者造成教育或就业中断，因为该规则允许 J-1 非移民在正确提交的延期请求正在审理期间、在 J-1 非移民授权入境期限期满后最多 240 天内，继续从事与其交流项目相一致的就业和培训活动。DHS 指出，240 天规则对于大多数其他非移民（包括许多其他职业的成员，例如 H-1B、L-1、O-1 等）是相同的要求。DHS 还指出，USCIS 通常建议非移民在当前停留期满前的六个月内 [206] 申请 EOS，并且 240 天仅在当前停留期满后开始计算。


DHS 承认，如果 EOS 申请在 240 天后仍在审理中，J-1 非移民将无法继续就业。然而，在 EOS 请求仍在审理期间，J-1 非移民将能够以授权停留期留在美国。虽然这可能会导致行政成本和时间来适应更改，但 DHS 认为增强的国家安全和项目完整性超过了这些负担。此外，如前所述，J-1 非移民在某些情况下可以向 USCIS 寻求加急裁决 [207]。鉴于这些选项，DHS 不同意固定入境期限和提交 EOS 申请的要求会造成不当负担，以至于该规则会阻碍 J-1 交流访问者参与这些项目。


2. 重复流程与监管权力冲突


意见： 多位评论者表示担心，拟议规则将为寻求延期的 J-1 交流访问者创建一个重复的流程，并写道 DoS 已经为 J-1 延期制定了有效的流程。评论者表示，DoS 目前在批准所有 J-1 项目类别的项目延期方面拥有唯一权力，如果拟议规则最终敲定，将通过要求参与者向 USCIS 提交单独的 EOS 申请来使这种权力变得复杂。一位评论者要求澄清 DoS 将如何与 DHS 协调，以尽量减少签证签发、I-20/DS-2019 有效期和 EOS 批准之间的矛盾时间线。


评论者表示，针对 J-1 非移民的现有 D/S 框架包含严格的限制，这使得该规则没有必要。同样，评论者强调 J-1 交流访问者受到仔细监控，DSO 和 RO 定期向 DHS 和 DoS 报告包括地址更改、学业进展和当前就业详细信息在内的信息。一位评论者还指出，J-1 项目目前具有明确的最大项目长度、类别更改限制以及诸如两年回国居住要求和重复参与禁令等额外的保障措施，这些措施有效地阻止了长期滞留。一位 DSO 指出，DoS 法规足以实现 DHS 的目标，因为 J-1 项目包含 14 个不同的类别，其中许多根据 22 CFR 62.20(i)(3) 已经制定了监管时间限制，这使得该规则显得重复且不必要。


回应： DHS 不同意评论者关于该规则创建了不必要的重复流程的说法。值得注意的是，DoS 关于 J-1 非移民延长其项目的法规不同于 USCIS 裁决其 EOS 的要求。项目期是指非移民注册的学术或培训项目的官方持续时间（例如学位或课程的开始和结束日期）；停留期是非移民获准在美国停留的时间长度，根据其签证身份的允许，可能包括项目期之前、期间和之后的时间。


虽然 RO 的推荐和 DoS 对项目延期的批准可能是支持 EOS 的一个因素，但移民官员还会审查与外籍人员移民身份相关的因素，包括可能基于背景调查触发的任何犯罪记录和可驱逐事由。RO 不是移民官员，缺乏授予 EOS 的法定和监管权力。本规则下的 EOS 流程将为移民官员提供一个机制，以在合理频繁的间隔内进行此项评估。DHS 期望此改变能够震慑和防止欺诈。


为了启动 EOS 流程，J-1 非移民首先必须根据 DoS 法规获得 RO 的推荐，并在适用的情况下获得 DoS 的批准。DHS 在规则中明确规定，EOS 的授予可受制于 DoS 关于项目延期的法规，前提是 DoS 批准项目本身的延期。参阅新 8 CFR 214.2(j)(1)(iv)(C)（“[s]ubject to the restrictions in the regulations at 22 CFR part 62, extensions of stay may be granted for a period up to the length of the program, as listed on the Form DS-2019, or successor form, not to exceed 4-years, unless the J-1 exchange visitor is otherwise restricted by regulations at 22 CFR part 62.”）。具体而言，DoS 法规规定 RO 可以将 J-1 交流访问者参与交流访问项目的期限延长至外籍人员特定项目类别授权的允许参与期限的限度。参阅 22 CFR 62.43。然后，RO 签发一份正式填写的 Form DS-2019，反映延期情况，并将该表格的通知副本提供给 DoS。参阅 22 CFR 62.43(a)-(b)。在某些项目类别中，对于特殊或不寻常情况，经 DoS 批准，允许超过最大项目持续时间的延期。为了获得此类延期的批准，RO 必须以参与者的名义向 DoS 提交一份电子请求，以证明请愿的合理性并提供支持文件。参阅 22 CFR 62.43(c)。此后，根据这项新规则的要求，J-1 非移民需要向 USCIS 申请 EOS。如果 DoS 拒绝延长交流访问项目，非移民将没有任何依据向 USCIS 提出 EOS。USCIS 将拒绝该呈请，因为不存在有效的基础项目。


总之，由于两个部门的独有职责，DoS 和 DHS 的流程和系统必然不同。本规则建立的流程为 DHS 提供了震慑和防范欺诈的机制，因此有必要实施。在 D/S 框架下，除非外籍人员申请变更为其他非移民身份或向 USCIS 申请其他福利，否则不需要背景调查。这就造成了一种情况：处于 D/S 中的非移民可以在美国停留很长时间，而无需接受美国政府以 EOS 申请形式进行的审查，包括随附的背景调查、随后的签证申请或在 POE 的检查，因为在 D/S 框架下的项目延期可以通过向 DSO 或 RO 申请来获得。识别并解决这一漏洞是本规则国家安全目标的关键部分，对于增加美国政府对这些外籍人员的监督和监控至关重要。


3. 对美国外交关系和知识交流的影响


意见： 评论者表示，“一刀切”的准入期限制可能会阻碍非移民参与文化和教育交流项目。评论者表示，拟议规则将对美国利益产生负面影响，并减少建立善意和相互理解的交流项目。评论者断言，对 J-1 项目的拟议修改通过阻碍国际参与并对学者的停留施加人为限制，与美国加强其经济和智力资本的目标相冲突，标志着对国家致力于相互理解和知识共享的承诺的退缩。


一位评论者建议，DHS 重新考虑对 J-1 签证条款的拟议修改，并维持支持该项目成功的最新政策变化。评论者解释说，历史上 J-1 签证促进了文化和教育交流，并且取消来自包括中国、印度和韩国在内的 34 个国家的 J-1 签证持有者的两年回国实际居住要求，代表着朝着加强国际合作迈出的重要一步，并提高了美国留住全球人才的能力。评论者写道，恢复或修改这项要求可能会阻碍潜在的交流访问者，特别是那些最近从 DoS 技能清单中移除的国家的人，并进一步断言此类更改可能会降低美国机构在吸引顶级国际学者和研究人员方面的竞争力。


回应： DHS 感谢这些关切。然而，DHS 无意让该规则导致外国学生参与 J-1 项目的显著下降，也无意导致与外国国民相关的人才、技术创新或经济效益的显著减少。DHS 断言，美国将继续吸引有才能的外国交流学生、学者、研究人员和医师，因为美国机构提供了高质量的教育。有关参与 J-1 项目这一问题的进一步讨论，请参阅上文第 IV.K.1 节《对所有 J-1 项目的普遍影响》。


DHS 还指出，拟议规则改变了 J-1 的入境方式以及他们延长停留的方式，但它不影响两年回国居住要求。如果 J-1 受制于 INA 212(e)，无论本规则下的新入境程序如何，他们在获得某些移民福利之前，仍必须履行该要求或获得该要求的豁免。


4. J-1 非移民身份的宽限期


意见： 几位评论者表示应维持或延长 J-1 宽限期。此外，一位评论者表示 J-1 宽限期应延长至 60 天。另一位评论者对拟议规则下 30 天宽限期的适用性和资格要求表示关切，并要求澄清未能完成学术目标的 J-1 非移民将会发生什么。


回应： DHS 已经考虑了该规则对 J-1 非移民医师、教师和其他交流访问项目类别的影响。DHS 认为该规则不会阻碍 J-1 交流访问者参与交流项目。DHS 不同意 30 天的宽限期对 J-1 非移民来说是不充足的宽限期，并拒绝将期限延长至 60 天。事实上，本规则之前的法规规定了 30 天的离境期，本规则并未改变这一点。参阅 8 CFR 214.2(j)(1)(ii) (2025)。未能按时完成项目且需要额外时间来完成其学术目标的 J-1 非移民可以向 USCIS 申请 EOS，由 USCIS 进行裁决。此外，DHS 指出，只要 J-1 非移民在 I-94 到期（包括 30 天宽限期）之前提交 EOS，其延期请求将被视为及时提交，并且 J-1 非移民将能够根据新 8 CFR 214.2(j)(vii) 中概述的要求继续其 J-1 授权活动。上文第 IV.K.1 节也讨论了允许的活动。


5. J-2 家属


意见： 评论者对 J-2 家属也需要申请延期的要求表示关切。一位评论者表示，如果该规则最终敲定，拟议规则并未明确 J-1 家属的入境将如何受到影响。评论者要求澄清家属的入境是否自动与主申请人的签证批准绑定。


回应： J-2 配偶和 21 岁以下未婚子女的初始授权入境期不得超过主 J-1 交流访问者的授权入境期。同样，J-2 家属的 EOS 不得超过主 J-1 交流访问者的授权入境期。参阅新 8 CFR 214.2(j)(1)(ii)(B) 和新 8 CFR 214.2(j)(1)(iv)(E)。J-2 家属的 EOS 申请可以包含在 J-1 非移民的 EOS 申请中，或者 J-2 非移民必须提交他们自己的 EOS。在 EOS 申请中，J-2 非移民必须证明他们正在维持合法身份且未从事未授权工作。此外，如果 J-1 非移民的 EOS 请求被拒绝，J-2 非移民的 EOS 也将被拒绝。
