English Original

6. Specific J-1 Programs a. Medical Training, Healthcare Programs, and Healthcare Workers Comments: Commenters stated the rule would significantly disrupt medical training and the clinical coverage that J-1 physicians provide across the United States. Commenters also pointed to the shortage of healthcare workers in the United States in various fields and raised concerns on the potential impacts of the rule to the U.S. healthcare system, including decreasing participation of physicians in the J-1 program. Many commenters emphasized that international physicians are essential to the U.S. healthcare system with one citing to statistics from a J-1 sponsor.[208] Specifically, commenters stated that international medical graduates (IMGs) constitute approximately 25 percent of the current physician workforce and play a vital role in addressing these shortages. Commenters also noted that approximately 17,000 J-1 physicians currently provide essential patient care across more than 770 teaching hospitals in 49 states, the District of Columbia, and Puerto Rico, and that more than 4,000 J-1 physicians enter U.S. residency and fellowship programs each year. Commenters mentioned that IMGs, including practitioners and trainees, represent large numbers particularly in the specialty fields, such as nephrology, endocrinology, psychiatry, psychology, geriatrics, infectious diseases, Alzheimer's and cancer research, epidemiology, biomedical sciences, and clinical research. A commenter wrote that infectious disease physician care is especially important especially for HIV research/care, and that almost a third of infectious disease physicians come from other countries, including those on J-1 visas. In addition, some commenters added that J-1 physicians disproportionately serve in primary care specialties experiencing the most severe shortages, including internal medicine, pediatrics, and family medicine. An individual who identified as a physician, citing the ECFMG, noted that this rule would impact over 15,000 J-1 physicians in residency programs nationwide, three out of four of whom are J-1 residents in a primary care specialty (Internal Medicine, Family Medicine or Pediatrics), with greater concentrations in hospitals and communities experiencing shortages. Similarly, a commenter expressed concern that the proposed rule, if finalized, would have a devastating impact on the health care of children, the training of the future pediatric subspecialty physician workforce, and future scientific research in the field. Another commenter stated that the proposed rule could cause a nursing shortage as foreign students play a vital role in caring for U.S. citizens. Another commenter noted that the rule poses a threat to preparedness for future pandemics and health crises. Additionally, commenters stated that J-1 physicians do not displace U.S. graduates but rather fill residency positions that would otherwise remain vacant. On that same note, commenters stated that foreign physicians frequently work in rural and underserved communities where healthcare access is already limited and bring valuable multilingual skills and cultural competence to care teams. In a joint submission, a program sponsor and a healthcare provider explained that in hard-to-staff regions, shortages form the basis for Health Professional Shortage Areas (HPSA) and Medically Underserved Areas (MUA) designations, and that many of these communities, particularly in rural areas, are unable to fill positions with American medical graduates. The same commenters emphasized that the current J-1 waiver program acknowledges this problem by allowing each state a limited number of waivers to address severe workforce shortages and warned that any disruption to existing training pathways would further exacerbate critical subspeciality shortages, including pediatric nephrology, neurology, endocrinology, infectious disease, and child/adolescent psychiatry. Along these lines, some commenters added that because some communities often cannot fill positions with American medical graduates, the Conrad 30 program and other J-1 waiver programs that direct physicians to underserved areas after completing their training are essential for addressing physician shortages in rural and underserved communities. A commenter also added that J-1 physicians predominantly train and serve in hospitals located in HPSA and in regions identified as medium to high-vulnerability by the CDC's Social Vulnerability Index. In sum, commenters warned that the proposed rule would make it more difficult for foreign physicians to study and provide care in rural and underserved communities, potentially worsening the projected physician shortage, resulting in reduced access to care, longer wait times, and increased costs, and undermining patient care across the United States. The commenters opined that these issues will arise due to the various requirements of the new rule. With respect to EOS requirements, commenters expressed concern about the potential for delays in processing EOS applications, which could prevent physicians from beginning or continuing their training on time. A commenter remarked that navigating extension applications during clinical rotations would create dangerous interruptions in patient care continuity and disrupt the training pipeline. Another commenter noted that current USCIS processing times for EOS range from 6 to 19 months, far longer than the 3- to 4-month window between Match Day and residency start, which could cause disruptions during the Match cycle. The commenter added that there is not currently an option to expedite or “premium process” an EOS application, and even if there were, such an option would be cost-prohibitive for J-1 physicians who are only paid a stipend. Commenters also noted the timeline constraints for filing EOS, stating that J-1 physicians currently must apply for continuation of sponsorship annually to ensure compliance with regulatory requirements and that IMGs are currently in accredited, monitored, and intensely regimented training programs. They cautioned that the proposed EOS requirement would incentivize program sponsors to issue DS-2019s for longer periods to reduce costs, potentially decreasing sponsor checkpoints for status maintenance—an outcome contrary to the rule's stated policy goals. Relatedly, commenters also stated that the proposed rule and the imposed timelines would destabilize graduate medical education programs across the United States. They explained that residency and fellowship training operate on precise annual cycles, with contracts issued just months in advance of the July start date. Commenters reasoned that residency and fellowship contracts are typically issued only a few months before the academic year begins on July 1, creating what they described as “an impossible timeline” for compliance. Commenters added that this would occur annually, potentially preventing thousands of J-1 physicians from continuing their training programs on time, further straining an already stressed healthcare system. Another commenter stated that even brief disruptions to J-1 status could force hospitals to stop treating patients mid program, jeopardizing continuity of care in hospitals and clinics. Further underscoring the length of medical training, many commenters stated that the proposed four-year limit is incompatible with many medical training pathways, which routinely extend beyond this proposed timeframe; completing a fellowship in addition to a residency could take five to seven years. Specifically, commenters stated that the fixed admission periods in the proposed rule would not allow J-1 physicians to complete their standard residency programs that lead to certification in specialties that require longer training periods. Multiple commenters also stated that the proposed rule would make fellowship trainings nearly impossible for many J-1 physicians, and that preventing clinicians from pursuing lateral training weakens cross-functional literacy on care teams. Commenters raised particular concern for medical specialty and subspecialty training programs, which can last from one to 7 years depending on the specialty. Commenters noted that while most residency programs require a minimum of 3 years of training, some specialties require as many as 7 years, which would directly conflict with the proposed 4-year limit. Commenters remarked that cardiologists and other medical specialists typically train for 3 years and then a 3-year fellowship and therefore, they would be disincentivized to continue training with uncertainty for an extension. Along those lines, a commenter pointed that certain neurology training pathways, especially child neurology programs, often exceed the proposed four-year limit, and thus, the rule would inhibit completion of neurological medical training, exacerbating the current shortage of neurologists. Similarly, a commenter stated that most physicians seek subspecialty training beyond their 4 years of residency training, including most medical and surgical specialties. Another commenter wrote that the proposed four-year limitation fundamentally misaligns with the structure of medical education, which typically requires six to eight years to complete residency and fellowship training. A professional association further stated that this is particularly significant as recent survey data indicates that 93 percent of adult neurology residents and 56 percent of child neurology residents report plans to pursue fellowship training. Lastly, several commenters noted that the proposed rule would impact J-1 physicians who have already met the maximum period of admission for J-1s but have not yet completed their training and therefore are not eligible for the H-1B lottery. Response: DHS agrees that J-1 health professionals provide an important service to the United States and has carefully considered the effects of the rule on J-1 nonimmigrant physicians and the U.S. healthcare system. However, DHS does not believe that this rule will discourage J-1 exchange visitors from participating in medical training in the United States or make the program cost-prohibitive. First, DHS acknowledges the concerns regarding delays in EOS adjudications and potential disruptions while the EOS application is pending. However, DHS notes that the rule allows J-1 nonimmigrants who have timely filed for EOS (which USCIS generally recommends that applicants file within six months preceding the expiration of their current stay [209] ) to continue employment and training activities, consistent with the exchange program, for up to 240 days beyond the expiration of their authorized period of admission. Secondly, as discussed previously in section IV.G.2.a. above, an applicant may request that USCIS expedite the adjudication of an application, including for an EOS, that is under USCIS jurisdiction.[210] DHS will continue to explore expanding premium processing for Form I-539 for the affected populations, including J-1 physicians. With respect to the commenter's concern that premium processing would be cost prohibitive for J-1 physicians who pay a stipend, DHS recognizes that aliens have varied ability to pay fees associated with applications, petitions, and other requests. DHS notes that there is no requirement to request premium processing, and there is no cost to the alien associated with an expedite request. Therefore, DHS is not making a change responsive to this comment. However, DHS and DoS have made operational changes that will avoid the need for those professions ( e.g., alien physicians, professors, or research scholars) on a yearly contract from needing to apply for EOS every year. The DS-2019 will be filled out in more detail, and the free text field (question number 4) will not only list the name of the program but will now also list the full dates of the program. Question number 3 will continue to list the contract end dates. DHS (CBP and USCIS) will utilize the dates in question number 4 to determine the maximum duration of the J-1 nonimmigrant's stay up to 4 years subject to restrictions at 22 CFR part 62. No changes to the regulatory text or form are required to make this change. It will be accomplished through training. DHS believes this rule is necessary to improve monitoring of this population and support the integrity of the country's immigration system. As with F nonimmigrants, setting the length of the J nonimmigrant's specific program and requiring EOS extensions thereafter will establish a mechanism for immigration officers to vet these nonimmigrants at defined periods and determine whether the J-1 nonimmigrants are complying with the conditions of their classification. Specifically, requiring J-1 nonimmigrants to request an EOS will improve consistency of terms of admissions between nonimmigrant categories, enable stronger oversight by government officers who will review the nonimmigrant's requests, enhance the government's ability to effectively enforce the statutory inadmissibility grounds related to unlawful presence, and deter aliens and entities from engaging in fraud, abuse, espionage and other criminal activities within these nonimmigrant programs. While the rule may have some impact in USCIS processing times, such is the burden of robust enforcement of the law, and DHS believes that the benefits of increased contact with the relevant populations of nonimmigrants outweigh processing time considerations. In sum, these proposed changes would provide the government with additional protections and mechanisms to exercise the oversight necessary to vigorously enforce our nation's immigration laws and protect the integrity of the immigration system. Comments: Commenters stated that the proposed rule is unnecessary for the J-1 physician program because it is already subject to strict requirements and regulated by DoS and non-governmental entities, such as the ECFMG, ACGME, and monitored in SEVIS. Commenters added that physician trainees are unlike other J-1 categories because training occurs in structured, accredited, and already heavily monitored programs, including accreditation oversight, continuous federal tracking and reporting, ongoing national and institutional oversight, and annual compliance renewals. On that note, commenters emphasized that J-1 physicians are among the most vetted visa holders. Specifically, commenters explained that J-1 physicians must earn the ECFMG Certification and undergo national security vetting, identity checks, and fraud-prevention screening before obtaining their J-1 visa. Some commenters added that the proposed rule would only introduce a duplicative review of J-1 physicians, given that the J-1 sponsor, ACGME accredited programs, and DS-2019 monitoring already provide significant oversight for J-1 physicians. A commenter mentioned that DHS should recognize J-1 sponsorship by a non-governmental organization as sufficient evidence of compliance to eliminate duplicative DHS adjudication and administrative burden. Commenters also added that the rule would not improve compliance but instead increase the risk of disruptions to training and patient care in the healthcare system. Response: DHS acknowledges the commenters' concerns. However, DHS disagrees that the current system provides sufficient oversight over the J-1 physicians and that recognizing the work of J-1 sponsors would be an effective mechanism to prevent fraud and abuse of the nation's immigration system. Generally, DHS does not have the authority to delegate its immigration responsibilities to non-government organizations, even where J-1 is concerned. Furthermore, permitting non-government organizations to conduct national security and fraud prevention undermines the Federal Government's responsibility to ensure the national security of the United States meets certain standards. As explained earlier at length, see General Impacts on all J-1 Programs, sec. IV.K.1, DoS and DHS processes and systems are necessarily different due to the unique responsibilities of the two departments. The processes established in this rule provide DHS with mechanisms to deter and prevent fraud, and therefore, are necessary to implement. Recognizing and addressing the vulnerability in the D/S framework is a key part of this rule's national security goals and is vital to increase the U.S. government's oversight and monitoring of these aliens. Comments: Commenters recommended that DHS create exceptions, preserve the D/S system, or alternatively lengthen the period of admission to be longer than 4 years for J-1 physicians without requiring repeated USCIS filings, to avoid any disruptions in their training and employment. Some commenters in particular sought exceptions for certain J-1 physicians, such as physicians completing their accredited medical training programs and medical residents and fellows in ACGME-accredited program. Specifically, with respect to lengthening the admission period, many commenters recommended that the period of admission align with the full length of their approved training, residency, or fellowship program, as documented on the Form DS-2019, or with the DoS recognized length of U.S. medical training, such as 7 years for physicians, to avoid mid-program EOS applications and ensure uninterrupted medical education and patient care. Another commenter stated that full program length admissions should include an additional 6-month period to accommodate for medical exams, personal leave, and other aspects of graduate medical education. One recommended a longer transition period for J-1 physicians to accommodate July start dates, licensure, and credentialing. A commenter specifically suggested a 120-day departure period for health-sciences graduates to allow sufficient time for licensure scheduling and relocation to clinical positions or residencies. Another commenter recommended that DHS revise the rule for longer admission periods to allow second master's program opportunities in healthcare fields. Several commenters suggested that DHS adopt a more targeted approach by establishing enhanced SEVIS analytics to identify overstay patterns, create streamlined renewal processes, and develop bilateral agreements with key partner countries for expedited processing. Similarly, a commenter recommended implementing enhanced SEVIS reporting requirements for medical schools and teaching hospitals, reasoning that this targeted approach would maintain oversight capabilities while protecting the integrity of medical training programs essential to American healthcare delivery. Some commenters also recommended that DHS streamline COS and EOS adjudications for J-1 physicians, and medical physicist/other healthcare professionals, such as waiving biometric requirements and interviews for EOS filings to reduce administrative burden. One commenter also added EOS expedited adjudications for J-1 physicians pursuing a subspecialty. A commenter called for a clear, streamlined mechanism for program-based extensions that avoids unnecessary delays and ensures uninterrupted training and patient care if DHS moves forward with the rule. Relatedly, other recommendations included changes to provisions relating to the 240-day rule in the context of pending EOS applications. Some commenters requested that DHS allow J-1 physicians to continue their employment activities beyond 240 days or the entire period that the EOS is pending. Separately, an organization requested that DHS include specific provisions for J-1 physicians to enable them to change programs with the appropriately endorsed Form DS-2019, during the pendency of an EOS application. The organization also provided the following proposed amendment:“J-1 nonimmigrants who are employment authorized with a specific employer incident to status continue to be authorized for such employment, or any employment authorized by their designated J-1 sponsor on Form DS-2019, for up to 240 days under the existing regulatory provisions at 8 CFR 274a.12(b)(20), if their status expires while their timely filed EOS application is pending.” The commenter also requested that DHS modify Form M-274, Employer Handbook, to reflect the procedures relating to automatic extensions of work authorization for J-1 physicians. Lastly, a university program noted that if DHS proceeds with the rule, any changes to the J-1 program must include robust protections, including automatic extensions; expedited adjudication; clear safe-harbors; explicit exemption of clinical training programs from fixed-end-date requirements; sufficient notice and transition periods for training programs; and consultation with medical education stakeholders. Response: DHS took these comments into consideration and is sensitive to commenter's concerns that with the time it takes to process extensions at USCIS, the rule might have an impact on the ability of J-1 physicians to provide healthcare in the United States. However, DHS declines to carve out an exemption for J-1 physicians. As discussed above, in General Impacts on all J-1 Programs, sec. IV.K.1, J-1 nonimmigrants may request that USCIS expedite the adjudication of an application, including for an EOS, that is under USCIS jurisdiction.[211] With respect to allowing J-1 physicians to continue employment beyond the 240 days while a timely filed EOS is pending, DHS notes that the 240-day rule is the same requirement for most other nonimmigrants, including members of many other professions ( e.g., H-1B, L-1, O-1, etc.). On that note, this rule will create consistency by aligning the requirements with other nonimmigrant classifications. And similar to those other visa categories, USCIS generally recommends that applicants apply for EOS within the six months [212] preceding the expiration of their currently authorized period of stay. The goals of the rule, described above, extend to all J-1 nonimmigrants, including J-1 physicians. DHS also noted instances of national security risks in the context of J-1 nonimmigrants.[213] Because these instances occur in a variety of programs and educational levels, DHS does not believe that it would be appropriate to only apply a fixed period of admission for undergraduate students and not for other students and programs. For further discussion on the purpose and need for this rule, see secs. II.B. and III.C. Lastly, regarding SEVIS enhancement, DHS notes that SEVIS is being updated as a result of this rule. DHS will continue to work with different agency components to ensure that all information related to SEVIS records is accurate and up to date. DHS agency components are modifying interfaces to make sure that all relevant data related to the regulation is available not only to DHS components, but to DoS as well.

中文翻译

本节汇总了公众针对拟议规则中对 J-1 医疗培训、医疗保健项目及医务人员（IMGs）产生的负面影响的激烈反对意见，以及国土安全部（DHS）的官方回应：


1. 公众的主要担忧与反对意见：


* 对美国医疗系统和短缺地区的毁灭性打击： 国际医学毕业生（IMGs）占美国医师总数的 25% 左右，约 17,000 名 J-1 医生在全美 49 个州及特区、波多黎各的 770 多家教学医院中提供至关重要的患者护理，且多集中于内科、儿科、家庭医学等严重短缺的初级保健领域以及农村和医疗资源匮乏地区（HPSA/MUA）。规则带来的行政中断将直接威胁患者护理连续性。


* 培训周期长与 4 年期限不匹配： 医疗培训（住院医师加专科进修）通常需要 5 到 7 年，甚至长达 7 年以上（如神经科、儿童神经科、心脏病学等）。4 年的固定准入期和频繁的 USCIS 延期流程与医学教育的精确年度周期（如每年 7 月 1 日开始，合同提前几个月敲定）根本无法契合，将导致灾难性的时间线冲突。


* EOS 审理延误与重复审核： USCIS 目前审理周期长达 6 至 19 个月，而 Match Day 到住院医师开学之间仅有 3 至 4 个月。J-1 医生已经通过了 ECFMG 认证、ACGME 认可项目及 DoS 的重重严格监管，USCIS 的额外 EOS 审核属于冗余且代价高昂的重复流程。


2. DHS 的官方回应：


* 允许及时申请与 240 天自动延续： DHS 指出，只要及时提交 EOS（建议在到期前 6 个月内），J-1 非移民可在期满后继续工作和培训最多 240 天，且可向 USCIS 请求加急审理。


* 针对年度合同的运营调整（关键例外）： DHS 与 DoS 进行了操作层面的调整，避免了诸如外籍医师、教授或研究学者等每年一签合同的人员每年都需要向 USCIS 申请 EOS。DS-2019 表格的自由文本字段（第 4 项）将详细列出项目的完整日期，CBP 和 USCIS 将利用该日期直接判定最长可达 4 年的准入期，无需通过修改法规即可通过培训来实现。


* 拒绝豁免与重申监管必要性： DHS 拒绝为 J-1 医生设立豁免。DHS 强调，联邦政府不能将国家安全和防范欺诈的责任委任给非政府组织（如 ECFMG 或 ACGME），统一的固定期限审查对于打击各行业潜在的欺诈和安全风险是必不可少的。


详细翻译


6. 特定的 J-1 项目


a. 医疗培训、医疗保健项目和医务人员


意见： 评论者表示，该 rule 将显著扰乱美国的医疗培训以及 J-1 医师在全美提供的临床覆盖。评论者还指出美国在各个领域存在医务人员短缺，并对该规则对美国医疗系统的潜在影响表示关切，包括 J-1 项目中医师参与度的下降。许多评论者强调，国际医师对美国医疗系统至关重要，其中一位引用了某 J-1 赞助方的统计数据 [208]。


具体而言，评论者表示，国际医学毕业生（IMGs）约占当前医师总劳动力人口的 25%，在解决这些短缺问题方面发挥着至关重要的作用。评论者还指出，目前约有 17,000 名 J-1 医师在 49 个州、哥伦比亚特区和波多黎各的 770 多家教学医院中提供基本的患者护理，每年有超过 4,000 名 J-1 医师进入美国的住院医师和专科进修（fellowship）项目。评论者提到，IMGs（包括从业人员和受训人员）在专科领域占据了大量比例，例如肾脏病学、内分泌学、精神病学、心理学、老年医学、传染病学、阿尔茨海默病和癌症研究、流行病学、生物医学科学以及临床研究。一位评论者写道，传染病医师的护理尤其重要，特别是对于 HIV 研究/护理，并且近三分之一的传染病医师来自其他国家，包括那些持 J-1 签证的人。


此外，一些评论者补充说，J-1 医师不成比例地服务于面临最严重短缺的初级保健专科，包括内科、儿科和家庭医学。一位自称为医师的个人援引 ECFMG 指出，该规则将影响全国范围内超过 15,000 名住院医师项目中的 J-1 医师，其中四分之三是初级保健专科（内科、家庭医学或儿科）的 J-1 住院医师，在经历短缺的医院和社区中浓度更高。同样，一位评论者表示，如果拟议规则最终敲定，将对儿童医疗保健、未来儿科亚专科医师劳动力的培训以及该领域的未来科学研究产生毁灭性影响。另一位评论者表示，拟议规则可能会导致护士短缺，因为外国学生在照顾美国公民方面发挥着至关重要的作用。另一位评论者指出，该规则对未来流行病和健康危机的防范构成了威胁。


此外，评论者表示，J-1 医师不会取代美国毕业生，而是填补了原本会空缺的住院医师职位。基于同样的思路，评论者表示，外国医师经常在医疗访问已经受限的农村和医疗资源匮乏社区工作，并为护理团队带来宝贵的多语言技能和文化胜任力。在一个联合提交的意见中，项目赞助方和医疗保健提供者解释说，在 staffing 困难的地区，短缺构成了卫生专业短缺区（HPSA）和医疗资源短缺区（MUA）认定的基础，其中许多社区（特别是在农村地区）无法用美国医学毕业生填补职位。相同的评论者强调，当前的 J-1 豁免项目承认了这一问题，允许每个州拥有有限数量的豁免来解决严重的劳动力短缺问题，并警告说，对现有培训路径的任何干扰都将进一步加剧关键的亚专科短缺，包括小儿肾脏病学、神经病学、内分泌学、传染病学以及儿童/青少年精神病学。顺着这些思路，一些评论者补充说，由于某些社区往往无法用美国医学毕业生填补职位，Conrad 30 项目和其他在培训结束后将医师引导至服务不足地区的 J-1 豁免项目对于解决农村和医疗资源短缺社区的医师短缺至关重要。一位评论者还补充说，J-1 医师主要在位于 HPSA 的医院以及被疾控中心（CDC）社会脆弱性指数识别为中到高脆弱性的地区进行培训和提供服务。总之，评论者警告说，拟议规则将使外国医师更难在农村和医疗资源匮乏社区学习和提供护理，可能恶化预计的医师短缺，导致护理获取减少、等待时间延长、成本增加，并破坏全美范围内的患者护理。


评论者认为，这些问题将由于新规则的各项要求而产生。关于 EOS 要求，评论者对 EOS 申请审理延误的可能性表示关切，这可能会阻止医师按时开始或继续其培训。一位评论者指出，在临床轮转期间导航延期申请将在患者护理的连续性中造成危险的中断，并扰乱培训管道。另一位评论者指出，当前 USCIS 对 EOS 的审理时间从 6 到 19 个月不等，远长于 Match Day 和住院医师开始之间的 3 到 4 个月窗口期，这可能会在 Match 周期中造成中断。评论者补充说，目前没有加急或“加急处理（premium process）”EOS 申请的选项，即使有，对于仅获得生活津贴（stipend）的 J-1 医师来说，这样的选项也将是成本 prohibitive 的。


评论者还指出了提交 EOS 的时间线限制，表示 J-1 医师目前必须每年申请延续赞助，以确保符合监管要求，并且 IMGs 目前处于经认证、监控且受到高度管制的培训项目中。他们警告说，拟议的 EOS 要求将激励项目赞助方签发更长周期的 DS-2019 以降低成本，从而可能减少用于维持身份的赞助商检查点——这一结果与规则既定的政策目标背道而驰。相关地，评论者还表示，拟议规则和强加的时间线将破坏全美研究生医学教育项目的稳定。他们解释说，住院医师和专科进修培训按照精确的年度周期运作，合同在 7 月开始日期前数月才签发。评论者推理说，住院医师和专科进修合同通常在 7 月 1 日学年开始前几个月才签发，这造成了他们所描述的合规“不可能的时间线”。评论者补充说，这将每年发生一次，可能阻止数千名 J-1 医师按时继续其培训项目，进一步拉紧本已承受压力的医疗系统。另一位评论者表示，即使对 J-1 身份的短暂中断也可能迫使医院在项目中途停止治疗患者，危及医院和诊所的护理连续性。


进一步凸显医疗培训的长度，许多评论者表示，拟议的四年限制与许多医疗培训路径不兼容，这些路径通常超出了这一拟议的时间框架；除了住院医师之外，完成专科进修可能需要五到七年。具体而言，评论者表示，拟议规则中的固定入境期限将不允许 J-1 医师完成其标准的住院医师项目，这些项目通往需要更长培训周期的专科认证。多位评论者还表示，拟议规则将使许多 J-1 医师的专科进修培训几乎成为不可能，并且阻止临床医生追求横向培训会削弱护理团队中的跨职能素养。评论者对医疗专科和亚专科培训项目表示特别关切，根据专科的不同，这些项目可以持续一到七年。评论者指出，虽然大多数住院医师项目需要至少 3 年的培训，但一些专科需要多达 7 年，这将与拟议的 4 年限制直接冲突。评论者指出，心脏病专家和其他医疗专科医生通常接受 3 年培训，然后进行 3 年的专科进修，因此，由于延期的不确定性，他们将被剥夺继续培训的动力。顺着这些思路，一位评论者指出，某些神经病学培训路径，特别是儿童神经病学项目，往往超过了拟议的四年限制，因此，该规则将阻碍神经医学培训的完成，加剧当前的神经病学家短缺。同样，一位评论者表示，大多数医师在其 4 年的住院医师培训之外寻求亚专科培训，包括大多数医疗和外科专科。另一位评论者写道，拟议的四年限制从根本上与医学教育的结构不相符，医学教育通常需要六到八年才能完成住院医师和专科进修培训。一个专业协会进一步指出，这一点尤为重要，因为最近的调查数据显示，93% 的成人神经病学住院医师和 56% 的儿童神经病学住院医师报告了寻求专科进修培训的计划。


最后，几位评论者指出，拟议规则将影响那些已经达到 J-1 最长准入期但尚未完成培训、因此尚不具备 H-1B 抽签资格的 J-1 医师。


回应： DHS 赞同 J-1 医疗专业人员为美国提供了重要服务，并已仔细考虑了该规则对 J-1 非移民医师和美国医疗系统的影响。然而，DHS 认为该规则不会阻碍 J-1 交流访问者参与美国的医疗培训，也不会使该项目变得成本 prohibitive。


首先，DHS 承认关于 EOS 裁决延误以及 EOS 申请审理期间潜在中断的关切。然而，DHS 指出，该 rule 允许已及时提交 EOS（USCIS 通常建议申请人在当前停留期满前的六个月内提交 [209]）的 J-1 非移民继续就业和培训活动，与交流项目保持一致，在授权入境期限期满后最多可达 240 天。其次，如上文第 IV.G.2.a 节中所讨论的那样，申请人可以请求 USCIS 加急裁决在其管辖范围内的申请，包括 EOS [210]。DHS 将继续探索为受影响的人群（包括 J-1 医师）扩大 Form I-539 的加急处理（premium processing）。关于评论者关心的加急处理对于仅获得生活津贴的 J-1 医师而言成本 prohibitive 的问题，DHS 认识到外籍人员支付与申请、呈请和其他请求相关的费用的能力各不相同。DHS 指出，没有要求必须请求加急处理，并且与加急请求关联的外籍人员没有费用。因此，DHS 未作响应此评论的更改。


然而，DHS 和 DoS 已经做出了运营层面的更改，将避免这些职业（例如外籍医师、教授或研究学者）中持每年一签合同的人员每年都需要申请 EOS。DS-2019 将被更详细地填写，并且自由文本字段（第 4 个问题）不仅将列出项目的名称，现在还将列出项目的完整日期。第 3 个问题将继续列出合同到期日。DHS（CBP 和 USCIS）将利用第 4 个问题中的日期来确定 J-1 非移民最长可达 4 年的停留期限，但须遵守 22 CFR 第 62 部分的限制。进行此项更改不需要对法规文本或表格进行修改。它将通过培训来实现。DHS 认为该规则对于改善对这一人群的监控和支持国家移民系统的完整性是必要的。与 F 类非移民一样，设定 J-1 非移民特定项目的长度并在此之后要求 EOS 延期，将建立一个机制，供移民官员在确定的时期审查这些非移民，并确定 J-1 非移民是否遵守其身份条件。具体而言，要求 J-1 非移民请求 EOS 将提高非移民类别之间入境条款的一致性，增强政府官员的监督能力（他们将审查非移民的请求），增强政府有效强制执行与非法滞留相关的法定不得入境事由的能力，并震慑外籍人员和实体在这些非移民项目中从事欺诈、滥用、间谍和其他犯罪活动。虽然该规则可能会在 USCIS 审理时间上产生一些影响，但这正是强力执法所伴随的负担，并且 DHS 认为，与相关非移民群体接触增加的好处超过了审理时间的考量。总之，这些拟议更改将为政府提供额外的保护和机制，以行使必要的监督，从而大力执行我国的移民法并保护移民系统的完整性。


意见： 评论者表示，拟议规则对于 J-1 医师项目是没有必要的，因为它已经受到 DoS 和非政府实体（如 ECFMG、ACGME）的严格要求和监管，并在 SEVIS 中受到监控。评论者补充说，医师受训人员与其他 J-1 类别不同，因为培训发生在结构化、经认证且已经受到沉重监控的项目中，包括认证监督、持续的联邦追踪和报告、持续的国家和机构监督以及年度合规续签。基于这一点，评论者强调 J-1 医师是受审查最多的签证持有者之一。


具体而言，评论者解释说，J-1 医师在获得 J-1 签证之前必须获得 ECFMG 认证并经过国家安全审查、身份检查和防欺诈筛查。一些评论者补充说，鉴于 J-1 赞助方、ACGME 认证项目和 DS-2019 监控已经为 J-1 医师提供了大量监督，拟议规则只会引入对 J-1 医师的重复审查。一位评论者提到，DHS 应当承认非政府组织对 J-1 的赞助足以作为合规证据，以消除重复的 DHS 裁决和行政负担。评论者还补充说，该规则不会改善合规性，反而会增加医疗系统中培训和患者护理中断的风险。


回应： DHS 承认评论者的关切。然而，DHS 不同意当前系统对 J-1 医师提供了足够的监督，也不同意承认 J-1 赞助方的运作将是防范我国移民系统欺诈和滥用的有效机制。通常，DHS 没有权力将其移民职责委任给非政府组织，即使涉及 J-1 也是如此。此外，允许非政府组织进行国家安全和防欺诈预防，破坏了联邦政府确保美国国家安全符合特定标准的责任。


正如先前详细解释的那样（参阅第 IV.K.1 节《对所有 J-1 项目的普遍影响》），由于两个部门的独有职责，DoS 和 DHS 的流程和系统必然不同。本规则建立的流程为 DHS 提供了震慑和防范欺诈的机制，因此有必要实施。识别并解决 D/S 框架中的漏洞是本规则国家安全目标的关键部分，对于增加美国政府对这些外籍人员的监督和监控至关重要。


意见： 评论者建议 DHS 创建例外情况、保留 D/S 系统，或者作为替代方案，延长 J-1 医师的准入期，使其长于 4 年，且无需重复提交 USCIS 申报，以避免其培训和就业中的任何中断。特别是，一些评论者寻求为某些 J-1 医师提供例外，例如完成经认证的医疗培训项目的医师以及 ACGME 认证项目中的医学住院医师和专科进修医师。具体而言，关于延长准入期，许多评论者建议准入期与 Form DS-2019 上记录的其批准的培训、住院医师或专科进修项目的全长相一致，或者与 DoS 认可的美医疗培训长度相一致（例如医师为 7 年），以避免项目中途的 EOS 申请，并确保不间断的医学教育和患者护理。另一位评论者表示，全项目长度准入应包括额外的 6 个月期限，以适应医学考试、个人请假和研究生医学教育的其他方面。一位建议为健康科学毕业生延长过渡期以适应 7 月份的开始日期、执照颁发和凭证授予。一位评论者特别建议为健康科学毕业生提供 120 天的离境期，以为执照排期和重新安置到临床职位或住院医师岗位留出足够的时间。另一位评论者建议 DHS 修改规则以延长准入期，以便在医疗保健领域提供第二个硕士项目机会。


几位评论者建议 DHS 采用更有针对性的方法，通过建立增强的 SEVIS 分析来识别逾期滞留模式，创建简化的续签流程，并与关键伙伴国家建立双边协议以进行加急处理。同样，一位评论者建议为医学院和教学医院实施增强的 SEVIS 报告要求，其推理是这种有针对性的方法将在维持监督能力的同时保护对美国医疗交付至关重要的医疗培训项目的完整性。


一些评论者还建议 DHS 简化 J-1 医师以及医学物理学家/其他医疗专业人员的 COS 和 EOS 裁决，例如免除 EOS 申报的生物识别要求和面试，以减少行政负担。一位评论者还补充说，应为追求亚专科的 J-1 医师提供 EOS 加急裁决。一位评论者呼吁，如果 DHS 向前推进该规则，应建立一个清晰、简化的基于项目的延期机制，以避免不必要的延迟并确保不间断的培训和患者护理。


相关地，其他建议包括修改在等待 EOS 申请期间与 240 天规则相关的条款。一些评论者请求 DHS 允许 J-1 医师在 240 天之后或 EOS 审理的整个期间继续其就业活动。另外，一个组织请求 DHS 包含针对 J-1 医师的具体条款，使他们能够在 EOS 申请审理期间，使用适当背书的 Form DS-2019 更改项目。该组织还提供了以下拟议修正案：“根据现行法规 8 CFR 274a.12(b)(20)，如果身份在及时提交的 EOS 申请审理期间过期，随身份附带特定雇主工作授权的 J-1 非移民继续此类工作（或其指定的 J-1 赞助方在 Form DS-2019 上授权的任何工作）的授权可长达 240 天。”该评论者还请求 DHS 修改 Form M-274《雇主手册》，以反映与 J-1 医师工作授权自动延长相关的程序。


最后，一个大学项目指出，如果 DHS 推进该规则，对 J-1 项目的任何更改必须包括强有力的保护措施，包括自动延长；加急裁决；清晰的安全港（safe-harbors）；明确豁免临床培训项目免受固定结束日期要求的限制；为培训项目提供充足的通知和过渡期；以及与医学教育利益相关者协商。


回应： DHS 考虑了这些评论，并且对评论者的关切很敏感，即鉴于 USCIS 处理延期所需的时间，该规则可能会影响 J-1 医师在美国提供医疗保健的能力。然而，DHS 拒绝为 J-1 医师开辟豁免。如上所述（在第 IV.K.1 节《对所有 J-1 项目的普遍影响》中），J-1 非移民可以请求 USCIS 加急裁决在其管辖范围内的申请，包括 EOS [211]。关于允许 J-1 医师在及时提交的 EOS 审理期间在 240 天之后继续就业，DHS 指出，240 天规则对于大多数其他非移民（包括许多其他职业的成员，例如 H-1B、L-1、O-1 等）是相同的要求。基于这一点，本规则将通过将要求与其他非移民类别相一致来创造一致性。并且类似于其他签证类别，USCIS 通常建议申请人在当前授权停留期满前的六个月内 [212] 申请 EOS。


该规则的目标（如上所述）扩展至所有 J-1 非移民，包括 J-1 医师。DHS 还指出了在 J-1 非移民背景下发生国家安全风险的实例 [213]。由于这些实例发生在各种项目和教育水平中，DHS 认为仅对本科生应用固定入境期限而不对其他学生和项目应用是不合适的。有关本规则目的和必要性的进一步讨论，请参阅第 II.B. 节和第 III.C. 节。


最后，关于 SEVIS 增强，DHS 指出 SEVIS 正在因该规则而进行更新。DHS 将继续与不同的机构组件合作，以确保与 SEVIS 记录相关的所有信息准确且最新。DHS 机构组件正在修改接口，以确保所有与规章相关的相关数据不仅可供 DHS 组件使用，也可供 DoS 使用。

English Original

b. J-1 Research Scholars and Academic Programs Comments: Commenters expressed concern about the negative impact this rule would have on professor and research scholar programs and academic training programs, including potentially disrupting collaborative research in various fields. Commenters noted that requiring exchange visitors in these programs to apply for program extensions through DHS could jeopardize grant-funded projects, as continuity of personnel is critical for meeting project timelines and deliverables. Commenters indicated that postdoctoral programs at research institutions would be especially impacted, some noting that domestic postdoctoral scientists typically favor higher-paid positions outside academia, but highly qualified international postdoctoral scientists are critical to advancing research, as many of them take positions in academic settings. Commenter noted that J-1 scholars often extend short-term research stays, and requiring USCIS approval for each renewal could delay or disrupt research advancements. Citing data from 2023, one commenter noted that Ph.D. degrees in Biological Sciences take an average of 5.8 years to complete. Additionally, the commenter noted that nonimmigrant visa holders made up 43 percent of doctoral students in science, engineering, and health, indicating that a substantial population would be impacted by the rule.[214] Relatedly, several UI/UX designers indicated that the rule would impact design projects, as product cycles—from initial user research, wireframing, prototyping, to usability testing and final rollout—could easily span more than four years and foreign students in multi-stage design-related STEM programs would be required to reapply for status during critical project milestones, causing interruptions. A university stated that the rule will increase demand from universities for H-1B and other visas/status changes, as exchange visitors would not be able to complete their allowed five years without applying for an EOS. The university stated that the burden of payment of the EOS application fee and uncertainty of approval for current J-1 exchange visitors would likely be dismissed in favor of a more stable status. The university predicted that intended researchers and post-docs may bypass the J-1 visa completely and opt to apply for H-1B or another visa for entry to the United States. Some commenters requested changes to the rule for J-1 nonimmigrants, including an exemption or a longer admission period for J-1 research scholars, short-term scholars, and professor categories, stating that these J-1 nonimmigrants participate in programs that run up to 5 years. Commenters explained that under this rule, these J-1 nonimmigrants would be required to request an EOS for the remaining year, which could lead to unforeseen troubles for the participants and require DHS to move quickly in processing these extensions. Similarly, another commenter asked that DHS specifically ensure that doctoral students are exempt from any fixed date policies, stating that the current D/S framework provides the necessary flexibility, efficiency, and stability for clinical and biomedical research training programs. Other commenters requested that DHS provide continuation of employment for J-1 researchers while timely EOS or related adjudications are pending. On the other hand, some commenters stated that J nonimmigrants should be admitted for only the specific program of assignment that they are involved in. The commenters noted that this period of admission should not exceed one year, ideally 6-12 months. Response: DHS appreciates the contributions of J-1 researcher scholars and reiterates that this rule does not prevent participation in J-1 exchange programs but rather sets a fixed period of admission with the opportunity to apply to extend one's stay, if required. As discussed previously in this section on J nonimmigrants, DHS does not expect that the rule will discourage or lower J-1 participation or have a significant impact on J exchange visitor programs. Alternatives to U.S.-based exchange visitor programs may be more difficult to find in other countries, providing less of an incentive for nonimmigrants to choose an alternative. Changing to a different visa category has always been an option and will continue to be an option; however, an alien and his or her employer will need to weigh the costs and uncertainty of approval in those categories against the costs and approvability of an EOS. Furthermore, as mentioned previously, DHS declines to adopt the suggestion that certain populations of students or exchange visitors, such as research scholars, be exempt from the fixed period of admission. As explained in the NPRM, DHS has identified troubling instances of J-1 nonimmigrants abusing the D/S framework and engaging in fraud and espionage. Therefore, DHS does not intend to exempt any visa classification from the fixed admission period framework. c. Summer Work Travel, Intern, Trainee, and Camp Counselor Programs Comments: Commenters expressed concerns about the impact of the proposed rule on the J-1 Summer Work Travel (SWT), camp counselor, intern, trainee, and other short term training programs, which DoS has already imposed time limitations. With respect to SWT, commenters stated that SWT is governed by 22 CFR 62.32, which limits participation to a maximum of 4 months during the student's academic summer break, with modest extensions. An NGO noted that SWT and camp counselor programs typically issue DS-2019 Forms that already cover the maximum duration for their categories; therefore, the rule will primarily impact the Au Pair, Intern, and Trainee categories. The NGO also noted that these categories already have a regulatory-mandated maximum duration, making indefinite extensions under the current D/S framework impossible. Commentators urged DHS to recognize the unique structure of short-term summer categories such as Camp Counselors and SWT and allow program date and sponsor adjustments. Relatedly, commenters also highlighted concerns about the Camp Counselor program, which limits the admission period to 4 months with no extensions. See22 CFR 62.30. One commenter noted that 32,000 international counselors participated in camps across the United States, indicating that the population impacted by the proposed rule is significant.[215] Another commenter requested that DHS clarify whether camp counselor sponsors may still issue an updated DS-2019 to accommodate “in-season adjustments” within the 4-month term, so long as the counselor's overall program length does not exceed regulatory limits. Other commenters expressed similar concerns about the J-1 Intern and Trainee programs which currently permit 12 months for interns and 18 months for trainees (22 CFR 62.22). Similarly, commenters expressed concerns about the impact of the proposed rule on the J-1 Secondary School Student Program (SSSP), which is regulated by 22 CFR 62.25. The commenters stated that the proposed rule, if finalized, would create financial burdens, reduce flexibility, and increase delays for EOS applications for SSSP participants. A professional association cited the J-1 SSSP as an example, noting that nearly 20,000 students participated in SSSP in 2024 under 22 CFR 62.25. The SSSP allows participants who enroll for one academic semester to extend to a full academic year through a streamlined, SEVIS-based process that does not require USCIS involvement. The professional association stated that the proposed rule would eliminate this flexibility by requiring a full USCIS extension filing, including a $420 fee, potential biometrics, and lengthy adjudication. Response: DHS acknowledges that these changes may result in additional costs and time for some nonimmigrants. However, DHS and DoS have made operational changes that will avoid the need for those J-1 visitors on annual agreements from needing to apply for EOS after every renewal. Sponsors will fill out the Form DS-2019 in more detail, and the free text field (field 4) will not only list the name of the program but will now also list the expected completion date of the full length of the program. Field 3 will continue to list the program end date, which aligns with the end date of the current agreement. DHS (CBP and USCIS) will utilize the dates in Field 4 to determine the maximum duration of the J-1 nonimmigrant's stay up to 4 years subject to restrictions at 22 CFR part 22. No changes to the regulatory text or form are required to make this change. It will be accomplished through training. DHS recognizes in-season adjustments and other desired changes to program duration may take place for some J-1 visitors who are admitted for a short duration, such as camp counselors, interns, trainees, and students in SSSP. If these visitors would like to remain in the United States under a J-1 visa for longer than their initial AUD, they will need to file an application for an EOS. Sponsors cannot make these adjustments. As discussed in this preamble and in the NPRM, DHS believes that any burdens and costs to certain J nonimmigrant visitors is outweighed by the benefits of this rule, which creates a mechanism for DHS to increase vetting, reduce overstays, and determine whether nonimmigrants are complying with the conditions of their nonimmigrant classification. The requirements imposed by this final rule provide the U.S. government more opportunities to review a nonimmigrant's records, admissibility, and any possible indicators of fraud, abuse, or national security concerns. Furthermore, fixed admission periods and filing an EOS application with USCIS are requirements for most other nonimmigrants who wish to enter or remain in the United States. DHS believes that the EOS requirement will allow DHS to vet nonimmigrants periodically to prevent and reduce issues of fraud, abuse, and national security threats.

中文翻译

本节汇总了公众针对拟议规则中对 J-1 研究学者、学术项目、暑期工旅游（SWT）、夏令营辅导员、实习生及培训生等项目产生的负面影响的意见，以及国土安全部（DHS）的官方回应：


1. 对 J-1 研究学者与学术项目的担忧与回应：


* 公众关切： 4 年期限限制和频繁的 USCIS 延期申请会打乱博士后、生物科学博士项目（平均需 5.8 年）及多年期跨学科/UI-UX 设计项目的稳定性，威胁 NSF 和 NIH 等联邦资助项目的进展；部分高校和研究机构可能因此放弃 J-1 签证，转而寻求 H-1B 等更稳定的身份。


* DHS 回应： DHS 重申该规则不禁止 J-1 参与，而是设定固定期限并提供延期途径。鉴于过去发现过 J-1 框架下的欺诈、间谍和滥用漏洞，DHS 拒绝为研究学者等群体提供豁免。


2. 对暑期工旅游（SWT）、夏令营辅导员、实习生及培训生等短短期项目的担忧与回应：


* 公众关切： SWT（最长 4 个月）和夏令营辅导员项目本身已有严格时间限制；该规则将主要对互惠生（Au Pair）、实习生（Intern）和培训生（Trainee）以及中学生交换项目（SSSP，近 2 万名学生）造成严重行政延误和经济负担（如 SSSP 延长学期原本只需通过 SEVIS 简便处理，新规下却需向 USCIS 提交完整申请并缴纳 420 美元费用）。


* DHS 回应： DHS 与 DoS 做出了操作层面的调整，避免了诸如每年一签合同的 J-1 访客在每次续签后都需要申请 EOS 的麻烦。赞助方将在 DS-2019 表格的第 4 栏中详细列出项目完整预期结束日期，CBP 和 USCIS 将据此核发最长 4 年的准入期。不过，对于中途的“季中调整（in-season adjustments）”，若需延长停留则仍需向 USCIS 申请 EOS。


详细翻译


b. J-1 研究学者和学术项目


意见： 评论者对该规则将对教授和研究学者项目以及学术培训项目产生负面影响表示关切，包括可能扰乱各个领域的合作研究。评论者指出，要求这些项目中的交流访问者通过 DHS 申请项目延期可能会危及资助项目，因为人员的连续性对于满足项目时间表和交付成果至关重要。评论者表示，研究机构的博士后项目将受到特别影响，一些人指出国内博士后科学家通常青睐学术界以外的高薪职位，但高素质的国际博士后科学家对于推进研究至关重要，因为他们中的许多人都在学术环境中担任职务。评论者指出，J-1 学者经常延长短期研究停留，每次续签都需要 USCIS 的批准可能会延迟或打乱研究进展。


引用 2023 年的数据，一位评论者指出，生物科学领域的哲学博士（Ph.D.）学位平均需要 5.8 年才能完成。此外，该评论者指出，非移民签证持有者占科学、工程和健康领域博士生总数的 43%，这表明相当大的人口将受到该规则的影响 [214]。相关地，几位 UI/UX 设计师指出，该规则将影响设计项目，因为从最初的用户研究、线框图、原型制作到可用性测试和最终推出的产品周期很容易跨越四年以上，并且多阶段设计相关 STEM 项目中的外国学生将被要求在关键项目里程碑期间重新申请身份，从而造成中断。


一所大学表示，该规则将增加大学对 H-1B 和其他签证/身份变更的需求，因为交流访问者在不申请 EOS 的情况下将无法完成他们获准的五年期限。该大学表示，EOS 申请费的支付负担以及对当前 J-1 交流访问者批准的不确定性可能会被抛弃，转而支持更稳定的身份。该大学预测，预期的研究人员和博士后可能会完全绕过 J-1 签证，选择申请 H-1B 或其他签证入境美国。


一些评论者请求对 J-1 非移民的规则进行修改，包括为 J-1 研究学者、短期学者和教授类别提供豁免或更长的准入期，并指出这些 J-1 非移民参与的项目运行期长达 5 年。评论者解释说，根据该规则，这些 J-1 非移民将被要求为剩余的一年请求 EOS，这可能会给参与者带来意料之外的麻烦，并要求 DHS 迅速处理这些延期。同样，另一位评论者要求 DHS 明确确保博士生免受任何固定日期政策的约束，并指出当前的 D/S 框架为临床和生物医学研究培训项目提供了必要的灵活性、效率和稳定性。其他评论者请求 DHS 在等待及时 EOS 或相关裁决期间为 J-1 研究人员提供就业延续。


另一方面，一些评论者表示，J 类非移民应仅针对他们参与的具体分配项目准入。评论者指出，此准入期不应超过一年，理想情况下为 6-12 个月。


回应： DHS 赞赏 J-1 研究学者的贡献，并重申该规则并未阻止参与 J-1 交流项目，而是设定了一个固定入境期限，并提供了在需要时申请延长停留的机会。正如本节关于 J 类非移民部分先前所讨论的那样，DHS 预计该规则不会阻碍或降低 J-1 的参与度，也不会对 J 类交流访问项目产生重大影响。在其他国家找到美国本土交流访问项目的替代方案可能更困难，从而减少了非移民选择替代方案的动机。更改为不同的签证类别一直是一种选择，并将继续是一种选择；然而，外籍人员及其雇主需要权衡这些类别中的成本和批准不确定性与 EOS 的成本和可批性。


此外，如前所述，DHS 拒绝采纳“允许某些学生或交流访问者群体（如研究学者）免受固定入境期限限制”的建议。正如在 NPRM 中所解释的那样，DHS 已经发现了 J-1 非移民滥用 D/S 框架并从事欺诈和间谍活动的令人不安的实例。因此，DHS 无意将任何签证分类豁免于固定入境期限框架之外。


c. 暑期工旅游、实习生、培训生和夏令营辅导员项目


意见： 评论者对拟议规则对 J-1 暑期工旅游（SWT）、夏令营辅导员、实习生、培训生和其他短期培训项目（DoS 已经对这些项目施加了时间限制）的影响表示关切。关于 SWT，评论者表示 SWT 受 22 CFR 62.32 管辖，该条款在学生的学术暑假期间将参与限制在最多 4 个月，并可进行适度的延期。一个非政府组织（NGO）指出，SWT 和夏令营辅导员项目通常签发的 DS-2019 表格已经涵盖了其类别的最大持续时间；因此，该规则将主要影响互惠生（Au Pair）、实习生（Intern）和培训生（Trainee）类别。该 NGO 还指出，这些类别已经拥有法规强制规定的最大持续时间，使得在当前 D/S 框架下的无限期延期成为不可能。评论者敦促 DHS 承认短期夏季类别（如夏令营辅导员和 SWT）的独特结构，并允许调整项目日期和赞助方。


相关地，评论者还强调了对夏令营辅导员项目的关切，该项目将准入期限制为 4 个月且不得延期。参阅 22 CFR 62.30。一位评论者指出，有 32,000 名国际辅导员参加了全美各地的夏令营，这表明受拟议规则影响的人口规模很大 [215]。另一位评论者请求 DHS 澄清夏令营辅导员赞助方是否仍可签发更新的 DS-2019 以适应 4 个月期限内的“季中调整（in-season adjustments）”，只要辅导员的总体项目长度不超过法规限制。


其他评论者对当前允许实习生 12 个月、培训生 18 个月的 J-1 实习生和培训生项目表达了类似的关切（22 CFR 62.22）。同样，评论者对拟议规则对受 22 CFR 62.25 管辖的 J-1 中学生交换项目（SSSP）的影响表达了关切。评论者表示，如果拟议规则最终敲定，将给 SSSP 参与者的 EOS 申请带来财务负担、降低灵活性并增加延误。一个专业协会以 J-1 SSSP 为例，指出 2024 年根据 22 CFR 62.25 参加 SSSP 的学生近 20,000 人。SSSP 允许注册一个学术学期的参与者通过一个无需 USCIS 参与的、基于 SEVIS 的简化流程延长至整个学年。该专业协会表示，拟议规则将取消这种灵活性，因为它要求提交完整的 USCIS 延期申请，包括 420 美元的费用、潜在的生物识别信息以及漫长的裁决过程。


回应： DHS 承认这些更改可能会给某些非移民带来额外的成本和时间。然而，DHS 和 DoS 已经做出了运营层面的更改，将避免那些按年签订协议的 J-1 访客在每次续签后都需要申请 EOS 的麻烦。赞助方将更详细地填写 Form DS-2019，并且自由文本字段（第 4 栏）不仅将列出项目的名称，还将列出整个项目全长的预期结束日期。第 3 栏将继续列出项目结束日期，该日期与当前协议的结束日期相一致。DHS（CBP 和 USCIS）将利用第 4 栏中的日期来确定 J-1 非移民最长可达 4 年的停留期限，但须遵守 22 CFR 第 22 部分的限制。进行此项更改不需要对法规文本或表格进行修改。它将通过培训来实现。


DHS 认识到，对于某些停留时间较短的 J-1 访客（如夏令营辅导员、实习生、培训生以及 SSSP 中的学生），可能会发生季中调整（in-season adjustments）和对项目持续时间的其它预期更改。如果这些访客希望以 J-1 签证在美国停留超过其最初的 AUD，他们将需要提交 EOS 申请。赞助方无法进行这些调整。正如本序言和 NPRM 中所讨论的那样，DHS 认为，对某些 J 类非移民访客的任何负担和成本都被该规则的好处所超过，该规则为 DHS 建立了增强审查、减少逾期滞留以及确定非移民是否遵守其非移民分类条件的机制。本最终规则所强加的要求为美国政府提供了更多机会来审查非移民的记录、入境资格以及欺诈、滥用或国家安全关切的任何可能指标。此外，固定入境期限和向 USCIS 提交 EOS 申请是大多数希望进入或留在美国的其他非移民的要求。DHS 相信，EOS 要求将允许 DHS 定期审查非移民，以防止和减少欺诈、滥用和国家安全威胁的问题。

English Original

N. I Nonimmigrants 1. Support Comments: A commenter endorsed the proposed changes regarding I nonimmigrants, stating that clearer definitions and stricter documentation requirements would ensure that I nonimmigrants are engaged in legitimate journalistic activities. I visa reform is necessary to prevent misuse of the visa category according to this commenter. Response: DHS agrees that the finalized changes to the I nonimmigrant classification are necessary to prevent misuse and ensure that I nonimmigrants are engaged in legitimate journalistic activities. 2. Purpose and Need Comments: Commenters stated that the NPRM did not cite any evidence that I-nonimmigrants pose a risk to national security and assert that the government did not provide other reasons or interests to support this change for I-nonimmigrants. Another commenter stated the only reason DHS gives for asserting that the term is appropriate is that it mirrors the automatic extension provided to I visa-holders whose current visa would otherwise expire because they intend to change mediums or employers. The commenter adds that it is illogical to think that the 240-day time intended for the government to determine whether an applicant is eligible for a visa is also enough time for the applicant to complete the activities the visa is intended to support. Citing 85 FR 27646, the commenter adds that the proposal is also inconsistent with the Department's previous view that visa terms of less than a year, coupled with the specter of non-renewal, can amount to “hostile measures targeting a free press” because of their disruptive effect on journalists' work. Response: With respect to I nonimmigrant visas, DHS recognizes the lack of public data cited in the proposed rule regarding fraud and overstays specific to I nonimmigrants. DHS notes that internal exit data show that the vast majority of I nonimmigrants stay for less than 240 days.[230] Therefore, for the I visa, this is an appropriate maximum timeframe. However, DHS disagrees with the general premise of these comments. The vulnerabilities generally associated with the D/S admission are not unique to F and J nonimmigrant classifications, and the lack of public data for the I nonimmigrant classification does not mean that the vulnerabilities are absent for that classification. As explained in the preamble of the NPRM and in this final rule, one of the goals of this proposed rule is to strengthen vetting and information collection and help immigration officers ensure that the I nonimmigrants are, and will be, engaged in activities that are permissible under INA 101(a)(15)(I), and do not pose national security issues (in addition to the F and J nonimmigrant classifications). As previously mentioned, DHS believes that admitting aliens temporarily in the United States for a fixed period will encourage aliens to maintain lawful status and prevent or reduce instances in which nonimmigrants unlawfully remain in the United States after their activities or assignments end. Through the EOS process, this rule takes both a proactive approach to prevent violations as well as a reactive approach to detect any ongoing violations. 3. Concerns and Practical Challenges of the 240-Day Limit Comments: Several commenters strongly opposed the proposed 240-day admission period for I visa holders. A commenter thought the 240 days was arbitrary compared to the new length of stay for students, which was based on the duration of a course of study. Other commenters stated that the proposed timeframe is inadequate for foreign journalists to effectively perform their duties. Commenters emphasized that foreign correspondents typically receive assignments in the United States lasting 3-to-5 years, and that the 3-to-4-year time frame allows I nonimmigrants to develop expertise, build source relationships, and gain cultural understanding necessary for quality reporting. Commenters reported a 240-day timeframe, therefore, is inadequate for covering long-term journalistic projects and events, including presidential election cycles, congressional sessions, extended sports seasons, investigative reporting, and ongoing coverage of evolving current events. Commenters noted that the proposed time period would create uncertainty, disrupt international travel, increase the risk of coverage interruption, and impair journalists' ability to develop deep knowledge and trusted networks. Commenters stated the proposed period of admission is not enough time for the applicant to complete the activities the visa is intended to support. Other commenters noted that fixed admission periods introduce uncertainty that will discourage participation in the United States. Commenters also stated there is a lack of evidence demonstrating a security risk, overstays, or misuse of the I visa under the D/S framework and, instead, there is strong oversight of I visa holders, with visas being linked to a named journalist and their employer. It was stated this oversight is stronger than when the I visa was first introduced. Commenters stated that the proposed rule would create substantial administrative burdens for foreign journalists and their employers. The commenters described concerns about the frequency of extension requests, repetitive documentation requirements, and risks associated with serial filings. One commenter estimated that more frequent I visa renewals would increase operational and travel costs by approximately 700 percent over a 5-year period, creating what they characterized as an unreasonable financial burden.[231] Additionally, another commenter noted that it is currently taking USCIS Service Center Operations more than 6.5 months to adjudicate 80 percent of EOS requests.[232] A commenter stated if an employer is not ready to immediately file an EOS, the lengthy processing times at USCIS could still result in a gap in work authorization, even with the automatic extension. Similarly, commenters highlighted numerous practical difficulties that the 240-day period would create for foreign journalists and their families. The commenters described that the 240-day period was too short to secure standard 12-month housing leases, arrange health insurance, develop professional resources, and obtain press credentials at institutions like the U.S. Capitol, and that it would create complications for obtaining and maintaining driver's licenses tied to visa status, disruptions to children's education, obstacles for accompanying spouses seeking employment, and overall instability to family members and dependents. One commenter noted that the proposed changes, if finalized, would limit the ability of international news organizations to recruit highly qualified journalists for assignment to the United States as many experienced journalists may be unable or unwilling to accept such a major change for a short period of time with no guarantee of extension. One commenter noted that, according to the Pew Research Center (2024), over 63 percent of Americans say foreign news coverage is important for democracy.[233] Another commenter expressed concern that the rule would weaken the ability of foreign correspondence to work in the United States, causing harm to the operations of international broadcasters and limiting international audience's from accessing independent reporting about the United States. Commenters also expressed concern that requiring frequent visa renewals would increase reliance on foreign intelligence, which they described as potentially politically motivated or inaccurate, leading to wrongful visa denials. They said the proposal to require thousands of experienced international journalists to submit and receive approval for visas is unrealistic, noting that the existing visa process already suffers from faulty foreign intelligence and incorrect automated flags that significantly delay applications. A commenter proposed a two-year period of admission for I nonimmigrants, either universally or by reciprocity, noting the existence of a reciprocity-based two-year admission framework for treaty investors (E-2). Response: DHS welcomes foreign journalists and is confident that the changes made by this rule balance journalistic needs with DHS's need for regularly determined points for verifying that a journalist maintains his or her status. As noted in the proposed rule, admitting I nonimmigrants for D/S affords them different treatment from most other nonimmigrants who are admitted for a specified period of time. As explained in the NPRM, DHS is adopting an established timeframe to which I nonimmigrants are already accustomed if they switch employers while on assignment in the United States.[234] While the commenters assert that the 240-day time frame is challenging and insufficient for many foreign correspondents to complete their assignments, DHS notes that internal exit data show that the vast majority of I nonimmigrants stay for less than 240 days.[235] Of those few I nonimmigrants who seek to complete longer assignments, they may apply for extensions as needed, to remain beyond 240 days (90 days for those presenting passports from the PRC, except for Hong Kong SAR or Macau SAR passport holders). For those who are assigned to a foreign news bureau's U.S. office for extended periods, other visas, such as the L-1 visa for managers and specialists, may be appropriate so long as the eligibility requirements for that category are met for that particular alien. Once an I nonimmigrant has timely filed an EOS, he or she may continue to work for the same employer for up to 240 days under 8 CFR 274a.12(b)(20) while the EOS is pending and is not required to leave the United States while the EOS is pending. As such, nonimmigrants will have their initial admission period, which is up to 90 or 240 days, and another 90 or 240 days if they timely file an EOS with USCIS and maintain lawful status (with the 90 days being applied to those presenting passports from the PRC, except for Hong Kong SAR or Macau SAR passport holders). See new 8 CFR 214.2(i)(5)(iii) and 8 CFR 274a.12(b)(20). DHS also notes, however, that should the journalist choose to travel outside the United States, he or she may be admitted by CBP, if admissible, for a period of time necessary to complete the planned activities or assignments consistent with the I classification, up to a 240-day period (or 90-day period for those presenting passports from the PRC, other than a Hong Kong SAR passport or Macau SAR passport) in that manner. DHS does not expect that the maximum 90- or 240-day admission period will deter or discourage foreign correspondents from coming to work in the United States. If an EOS application remains pending at the end of this 90- or 240-day period, the I nonimmigrant alien, whose status has expired, may remain in the United States, but not work, so long as the EOS application is pending, he or she has timely filed a subsequent EOS request to remain beyond the period requested in the preceding request, and he or she does not otherwise violate the terms of his or her authorized period of stay. See new 8 CFR 214.2(i)(5)(i)(C) and new 8 CFR 214.2(i)(5)(iii)(B). The I nonimmigrant alien, however, must cease working until their initial EOS application is approved. See new 8 CFR 214.2(i)(5)(i)(C). USCIS will deny the EOS application if the alien did not cease working after the 240-day period and before the EOS request was approved. See new 8 CFR 214.2(i)(5)(i)(C). DHS recognizes this rule could increase the need for I nonimmigrants to file EOS requests and may increase USCIS processing times. But, as outlined in other sections herein, such as Secs. III.C.4 and IV.G.1.c.(1), DHS believes the benefits of increased DHS contact with these nonimmigrants outweigh these burdens and processing time considerations. USCIS is fee funded and can make adjustments as appropriate. DHS also notes allowing an I nonimmigrant to continue working while both his or her initial and second EOS applications are still pending may be problematic, especially if the initial I extension request is denied, because that would mean that someone who was ineligible as an I nonimmigrant was able to continue working beyond the period when his or her initial EOS request is pending because of the adjudication timeframes and the nonimmigrant's ability to continue filing EOS requests.

中文翻译

本节汇总了公众针对拟议规则中对 I 类（外国信息媒体代表/记者）签证的影响提出的反馈意见，以及国土安全部（DHS）的官方回应：


1. 公众支持与质疑：


* 支持意见： 有评论者赞同对 I 类签证进行改革，认为更清晰的定义和更严格的档案要求有助于确保 I 类人员真正从事合法的记者活动，防止签证被滥用。


* 反对与质疑： 众多评论者和媒体代表强烈反对将 I 类非移民的初始准入期限制为 240 天（中国大陆公民为 90 天）。他们指出，外媒记者通常在美执行为期 3 至 5 年的长期报道任务（如覆盖总统大选、国会、长期体育赛事或深度调查报道），240 天的期限过短，无法建立消息来源或展开长期工作。频繁的续签将带来巨大的行政和差旅成本、住房租赁、医疗保险及子女教育等生活的不稳定性，甚至被视为对新闻自由的干扰。


2. DHS 的官方回应：


* 内部数据支撑期限： DHS 承认规则中未引述公开数据，但指出内部出境数据显示，绝大多数 I 类非移民在美停留时间短于 240 天，因此 240 天是一个合理的初始最大时间框架。


* 允许延期与工作授权延续： 若需执行长期任务，I 类人员可在到期前申请停留延期（EOS）。在及时提交 EOS 后的审理期间，他们可继续工作长达 240 天。若首个 EOS 在 240 天后仍在审理中，在未获批前虽不能继续工作，但可以合法留在美国等待裁决。


* 其他签证路径： 对于需要长期驻留美的新闻分社人员，DHS 建议符合条件者可考虑转用 L-1 等其他签证类别。DHS 坚信，平衡新闻需求与国家安全定点核查的益处远超行政负担。


详细翻译


N. I 类非移民


1. 支持


意见： 一位评论者赞同关于 I 类非移民的拟议修改，表示更清晰的定义和更严格的文件要求将确保 I 类非移民从事合法的记者活动。根据这位评论者的说法，I 签证改革对于防止签证类别被滥用是必要的。


回应： DHS 赞同对 I 类非移民分类做出的最终修改是防止滥用并确保 I 类非移民从事合法记者活动所必需的。


2. 目的与必要性


意见： 评论者表示，NPRM 未能引用任何证据表明 I 类非移民对国家安全构成风险，并断言政府没有提供其他理由或利益来支持对 I 类非移民的这一更改。另一位评论者表示，DHS 声称该期限合适的唯一理由是它反映了给予当前签证原本会因为打算更改媒体或雇主而到期的 I 签证持有者的自动延期。该评论者补充说，认为政府用来确定申请人是否有资格获得签证的 240 天时间也足够申请人完成签证旨在支持的活动的看法是不合逻辑的。引用 85 FR 27646，该评论者补充说，该提案也与该部门先前的观点不一致，即少于一年的签证期限加上不续签的阴影，由于其对记者工作的破坏性影响，可能构成“针对自由媒体的敌对措施”。


回应： 关于 I 类非移民签证，DHS 承认拟议规则中未引述有关 I 类非移民欺诈和逾期滞留的公开数据。DHS 指出，内部出境数据显示，绝大多数 I 类非移民的停留时间少于 240 天 [230]。因此，对于 I 签证而言，这是一个适当的最大时间框架。然而，DHS 不同意这些评论的一般前提。通常与 D/S 准入相关的漏洞并非 F 和 J 类非移民身份所独有，缺乏针对 I 类非移民的公开数据并不意味着该分类不存在这些漏洞。正如 NPRM 序言和本最终规则中所解释的那样，本拟议规则的目标之一是加强审查和信息收集，并帮助移民官员确保 I 类非移民现在和将来都在从事 INA 101(a)(15)(I) 准许的活动，且不存在国家安全问题（除 F 和 J 类非移民身份外）。如前所述，DHS 认为，临时准许外籍人员以固定期限入境美国将鼓励外籍人员保持合法身份，并防止或减少非移民在其活动或任务结束后非法滞留美国的实例。通过 EOS 流程，本规则采取了预防违规的主动方法以及检测任何持续违规的被动方法。


3. 240 天限制的关切与现实挑战


意见： 几位评论者强烈反对拟议的针对 I 签证持有者的 240 天准入期。一位评论者认为，与基于课程持续时间的新的学生停留期相比，240 天是武断的。其他评论者表示，拟议的时间框架对于外国记者有效履行职责来说是不够的。评论者强调，外国记者通常在美国接受持续 3 到 5 年的指派，并且 3 到 4 年的时间框架允许 I 类非移民培养专业知识、建立消息来源关系并获得高质量报道所需的文化理解。因此，评论者报告称，240 天的时间框架不足以涵盖长期的记者项目和事件，包括总统选举周期、国会会议、延长的体育赛季、调查性报道以及对不断演变的当前事件的持续报道。评论者指出，拟议的时间期将产生不确定性、扰乱国际旅行、增加报道中断的风险，并削弱记者发展深厚知识和受信任网络的能力。评论者表示，拟议的准入期不足以让申请人完成签证旨在支持的活动。


其他评论者指出，固定入境期限引入了不确定性，这将阻碍在美国的参与。评论者还指出，缺乏证据表明在 D/S 框架下存在安全风险、逾期滞留或滥用 I 签证的情况，相反，对 I 签证持有者的监督很强，签证与具名的记者及其雇主挂钩。据称这种监督比首次引入 I 签证时更强。


评论者表示，拟议规则将给外国记者及其雇主带来巨大的行政负担。评论者描述了对延期请求频率、重复性文件要求以及与系列申报相关的风险的关切。一位评论者估计，更频繁的 I 签证续签将在 5 年内将运营和差旅成本增加约 700%，从而造成他们所描述的不合理的财务负担 [231]。此外，另一位评论者指出，USCIS 服务中心运营目前需要超过 6.5 个月的时间来裁决 80% 的 EOS 请求 [232]。一位评论者表示，如果雇主没有准备好立即提交 EOS，USCIS 漫长的审理时间仍可能导致工作授权出现间隙，即使有自动延期也是如此。


同样，评论者强调了 240 天期限将给外国记者及其家属带来的众多现实困难。评论者描述说，240 天期限太短，无法获得标准的 12 个月房屋租赁、安排健康保险、开发专业资源以及在美国国会等机构获得记者证，并且这将给获得和维护与签证身份挂钩的驾驶执照带来复杂情况、扰乱儿童的教育、给寻求就业的随行配偶带来障碍，并给家庭成员和家属带来整体的不稳定性。一位评论者指出，如果最终敲定，拟议的更改将限制国际新闻机构招募高素质记者指派到美国的能力，因为许多有经验的记者可能无法或不愿意接受这种重大更改，且短期内没有延期保证。一位评论者指出，根据皮尤研究中心（2024 年）的数据，超过 63% 的美国人表示外国新闻报道对民主很重要 [233]。另一位评论者表达了担忧，认为该规则将削弱外国通讯社在美国工作的能力，损害国际广播机构的运营，并限制国际受众获取有关美国的独立报道。


评论者还对要求频繁续签签证会增加对外国情报的依赖表示关切，他们将其描述为可能出于政治动机或不准确，从而导致错误的签证拒绝。他们表示，要求数千名有经验的国际记者提交并获得签证批准的提案是不切实际的，并指出现有的签证流程已经遭受有缺陷的外国情报和不正确的自动标记的困扰，这大大延迟了申请。


一位评论者提议为 I 类非移民设定两年的准入期（通过普遍方式或对等方式），并指出存在适用于条约投资者（E-2）的基于对等的两年准入框架。


回应： DHS 欢迎外国记者，并确信本规则所作的更改在记者需求与 DHS 对定期确定核查记者是否维持身份的需求之间取得了平衡。正如拟议规则中所指出的那样，准许 I 类非移民以 D/S 入境使他们享受与大多数其他在指定时间内入境的非移民不同的待遇。正如 NPRM 中所解释的那样，DHS 正在采用一个 I 类非移民如果在美国指派期间更换雇主时已经习惯了的既定时间框架 [234]。虽然评论者断言 240 天的时间框架对许多外国记者来说是具有挑战性的且不足以完成他们的指派，但 DHS 指出，内部出境数据显示，绝大多数 I 类非移民的停留时间少于 240 天 [235]。对于少数寻求完成更长指派的 I 类非移民，他们可以根据需要申请延期，以停留超过 240 天（对于出示中华人民共和国护照的人为 90 天，香港特别行政区或澳门特别行政区护照持有者除外）。对于被指派到外国新闻分社美国办公室较长时间的人员，只要特定外籍人员符合该类别的资格要求，其他签证（如针对经理和专家的 L-1 签证）可能是合适的。


一旦 I 类非移民及时提交了 EOS，他或她可以根据 8 CFR 274a.12(b)(20) 继续为同一雇主工作长达 240 天，同时 EOS 正在审理中，且在 EOS 审理期间无需离开美国。因此，非移民将拥有其初始准入期（最长 90 或 240 天），如果他们及时向 USCIS 提交 EOS 并维持合法身份，则可再获得 90 或 240 天（90 天适用于出示中华人民共和国护照的人，香港特别行政区或澳门特别行政区护照持有者除外）。参阅新 8 CFR 214.2(i)(5)(iii) 和 8 CFR 274a.12(b)(20)。然而，DHS 还指出，如果记者选择在美境外旅行，如果具备入境资格，CBP 可以准许其入境，其时间长短为完成与 I 类身份相符的计划活动或指派所需的时间，最长可达 240 天（或对于出示中华人民共和国护照的人（香港特别行政区护照或澳门特别行政区护照除外）为 90 天）。DHS 预计最长 90 或 240 天的准入期不会阻碍或吓退外国记者来美工作。如果在这一 90 或 240 天期限结束时 EOS 申请仍在审理中，身份已过期的 I 类非移民外籍人员可以留在美国，但不得工作，前提是 EOS 申请正在审理中，他或她已及时提交了后续的 EOS 请求以留在前一请求中请求的期限之外，并且他或她没有以其他方式违反授权停留期的条款。参阅新 8 CFR 214.2(i)(5)(i)(C) 和新 8 CFR 214.2(i)(5)(iii)(B)。然而，I 类非移民外籍人员必须停止工作，直至其初始 EOS 申请获批。参阅新 8 CFR 214.2(i)(5)(i)(C)。如果外籍人员在 240 天期满后且在 EOS 请求获批之前没有停止工作，USCIS 将拒绝 EOS 申请。参阅新 8 CFR 214.2(i)(5)(i)(C)。


DHS 认识到该规则可能会增加 I 类非移民提交 EOS 请求的需求，并可能增加 USCIS 的审理时间。但是，正如本规则其他部分（如第 III.C.4 节和第 IV.G.1.c.(1) 节）中所概述的那样，DHS 认为 DHS 与这些非移民接触增加的好处超过了这些负担和审理时间的考量。USCIS 是靠收费资助的，并且可以适时进行调整。DHS 还指出，允许 I 类非移民在初始和第二个 EOS 申请仍在审理期间继续工作可能会带来问题，特别是如果初始的 I 类延期请求被拒绝的话，因为这意味着由于裁决时间框架以及非移民继续提交 EOS 请求的能力，原本不具备 I 类非移民资格的人能够在初始 EOS 请求审理期间继续工作。
